# GROUND STOP — internal creative map
FANFICTION: Tony Stark x Bruce Wayne, self-contained shared-continuity crossover.
Setting: Bellwether International Airport, fictional Port Mercer on US Atlantic coast; Terminal C, Gate C19, one severe November nor'easter; all plot happens from Tuesday ~17:12 to Wednesday ~12:40 (19h+). Roads closed by tidal flooding, flights grounded, conditions unsafe for armor flight. Grounded technology, no multiverse or magic.
POV: close third, past tense, alternating Tony and Bruce, occasional Leila late act. Genre: character-driven locked-terminal mystery with humor, action, no graphic gore.
THEME: Counting people is not the same as caring for them. Two men who built systems to protect others must learn to let ordinary people lead.
KEY PEOPLE: Leila Moreno (airport night operations director, former ramp worker, competent incident commander); Anika Bell (gate agent, uncompromising); Jonah Price (maintenance supervisor, approaching retirement); Dr. Nia Okafor (stranded ER doctor); Asha Desai (19-year-old airport systems apprentice); Frances 'Frankie' Bell (older custodian, husband Leo at home, no relation to Anika); Cass Finch (contractor Harbor Meridian director of risk operations, smart capable antagonist, pressured by contracts); Dorian Kestrel (airport deputy commissioner, prefers avoiding panic; not evil); children Nina Ortiz, 9, with aunt Celia; elderly passenger Malik Haddad requires oxygen; ferry/road closure.
TECHNOLOGY: Harbor Meridian's VELA airport resilience dashboard combines occupancy counts, badge scans, shelter maps, resource allocations. Dashboard undercounts people in Annex C ('the Gallery'), due a deliberately suppressed exception class in a contractually mandated demonstration; marks zone as cleared when not. This produces real danger as storm surges and air-handling shutoff; rear window drainage and backup ventilation issues. Avoid replicable exploit detail; focus on organizational failures/evidence and manual roll calls. VELA system not sentient or hacking AI. Cass knows suppression, uses storm to trigger rollback that destroys audit trail (not physical harm intent) and conceals unsafe routing. 
THE MYSTERY: mismatch between manual paper counts and screen; a ghost boarding call C0; 43 people moved during automatic protective relocation; missing custodian Frankie; missed oxygen carts; engineer notes indicate Annex C sealed while reporting vacant. Main causal source: undercount was preexisting from pilot design, not storm, which exposes it. Non-villain workers can solve. 
HERO RESOURCES: Tony has phone, improvised electronics but no suit, can get access to some non-sensitive tools under supervision; Bruce has no utility belt, relies on observation, people, planning; phone intermittent; Alfred and Pepper short cameos. Both do not override Leila's authority. 
THE CLOCK: 17:12 arrival/meeting, ~19:00 weather closure; 20:35 dashboard mismatch; 21:45 first missing sign; 00:20 annex discovery; 02:30 confrontation; 04:30 rescue onset; 06:15 data preservation; 08:40 storm wanes; 11:50 first flight ground stop lifts; 12:40 remain for closing.
ACT I: 1 The Other Man at Gate C19 2 Weather Minimums 3 A Number on a Screen 4 C Zero 5 The Room That Was Empty
ACT II: 6 A Paper Cup of Bad Coffee 7 The People in the Margin 8 An Audit of Small Things 9 The Founder Problem 10 Lost and Found 11 Thirty-Seven Minutes of Dark 12 The Break Between Men 13 Under the Sign
ACT III: 14 The Gallery 15 Who Owns the Emergency 16 Something That Can Be Carried 17 The Longest Night 18 The Price of an All-Clear 19 Departures 20 The Last Passenger
Epilogue: Two Letters, Nine Weeks Later.
RUBRIC: every chapter has a scene purpose, active decision, altered stakes, full dialogue, sensory grounding, distinct voice. No easy deus ex. Final staff/union/public authority get credit; billionaires fund improvements under conditions (no NDA). Preserve ongoing storm state, chronic fatigue, injuries, evidence chain, phone connectivity, power.
EOF

# Chapter One: The Other Man at Gate C19

Tony Stark had been delayed by hurricanes, volcanoes, coups, mechanical failure, diplomatic incidents, and once by a goat that had wandered onto a runway in a country whose aviation authority insisted the animal was an agricultural asset rather than an obstruction.

He had never been delayed by a printer.

The machine behind the service desk at Gate C19 had made the same noise for eleven minutes: a choked, tentative grinding, like an elderly robot reconsidering the value of work. Every few seconds the gate agent lifted its lid, studied the paper inside, closed the lid, and tried again. On the fourth attempt she stopped looking apologetic. By the sixth she had begun to look homicidal.

Tony respected that. Frustration was a respectable first stage of engineering.

"I could fix it," he offered.

"Sir, please don't touch the printer."

"Didn't say I would. I said I could. There is a meaningful distinction between capability and intent."

"Please don't demonstrate either one."

The woman was in her forties, with a silver streak at one temple and a brass name plate that read ANIKA BELL. She wore her airline scarf loose and possessed the focused authority of somebody who had spent twenty years explaining to people with expensive watches that the laws of physics did not have upgrade options.

Tony was beginning to like her.

His phone buzzed. A message from Pepper appeared over the airline's continually evolving estimates.

*Did you reach the car?*

*Technically,* he typed, *I have reached a chair. The car remains an aspirational concept.*

*Tony.*

*I'm fine. Weather. Terminal C. I am interacting respectfully with infrastructure.*

He put the phone away before the reply arrived. Certain accusations didn't merit the dignity of evidence.

Bell pulled a sheet of paper from the printer and held it at arm's length. It was blank.

"There," Tony said. "A perfect representation of the airline's current plan."

"Mr. Stark, if you could step aside—"

"He'd have to stop performing for the room," said a voice from behind him. "That would be difficult."

Tony turned.

Bruce Wayne stood six feet away with a battered leather overnight bag in one hand and a paper cup of black coffee in the other. In person he was broader than in photographs, not because the photographs lied but because he appeared to have a habit of occupying as little space as a large man could. His charcoal coat was expensive without advertising the fact. His expression suggested that he had been disappointed in the world before breakfast and had been gathering supporting documentation ever since.

Tony looked him up and down.

"Bruce Wayne. Well. This just became the world's least profitable convention."

"You weren't invited?"

"I meant the runway situation. Why are you flying commercial? Lose a bet?"

"I thought it might be educational."

"And?"

"I learned that printers aren't intimidated by celebrity."

Bell made an involuntary noise that was almost a laugh. Tony noticed. Bruce noticed Tony noticing. The first skirmish was decided without either man acknowledging a score.

Tony swept a hand toward the gate. Beyond the broad windows, rain traveled almost horizontally past floodlights and the hulking silhouettes of parked aircraft. A baggage tug moved through the storm with the dignity of a beetle attempting to cross the ocean floor.

"Just so we're all on the same page, C19 is a departure gate. It normally has a departure. That is sort of its job."

"The airport's job," Bell replied, "is to get you where you're going safely. The gate's job is not to argue."

"Then one of us is underemployed."

Bruce took a seat in the row nearest the windows. He placed his bag between his shoes, not beside him. Tony clocked the movement instantly. It left Bruce's hands free, kept his belongings controlled, and blocked no aisle.

"Who taught you to sit like that?" Tony asked.

Bruce sipped his coffee. "Someone who disliked tripping hazards."

"Must have been a fascinating childhood."

"Parts of it."

There was a silence. It wasn't long, but it was shaped in an unfamiliar way. Tony could fill a ballroom with words and had never entirely learned when leaving a few out might be kinder.

He sat one chair away.

"There's a resilience conference in Port Mercer," Tony said. "You were at the dinner."

"I was at the panels."

"Same thing, with less overpriced fish."

"The fish was adequate."

"That may be the bleakest sentence ever spoken by a billionaire."

Bruce looked toward the departures screen. Every line showed a variant of the same two words: DELAYED or HOLD. The smaller blue strip along the bottom of the display advertised weather updates with the confidence of a service that had not yet been asked to prove anything.

"They know how long?" Bruce asked Bell.

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because the airport can't tell the weather what to do."

"Is there a local estimate?"

"An updated one in twenty minutes."

Bruce nodded. "Thank you."

Tony stared. "That's it? That's the technique? Ask a question, get an answer, stop?"

"It's a method."

"Sounds suspiciously like surrender."

"You'd be surprised how much you learn when you stop talking."

Tony leaned back. He could see a faint reflection of himself in the glass: dark hair on the verge of needing a haircut, expensive clothes worn carelessly, a smile he had practiced until it worked even when he didn't want it to. Behind the reflection, the storm made a gray wall of the world.

"Port Mercer isn't supposed to have storms like this in November," he said.

"It does," Bruce replied. "It's having one."

"That was an observation, not a request for philosophical correction."

The loudspeakers crackled.

"Attention passengers. Bellwether International is experiencing severe weather conditions. All outbound flights are under a temporary ground stop pending review. Please remain inside the terminal and follow the instructions of airport personnel. We will provide additional information as it becomes available."

A low murmur rolled across the gate area. People reached for phones. A boy in a dinosaur sweatshirt pressed his face to the window until his mother guided him gently back. A businessman in a pale blue suit declared into a headset that there had to be *someone he could call.*

Bell did not look up from her desk. "There isn't," she said, almost to herself.

Tony's phone buzzed again. Pepper this time: *Don't make me call the airport.*

He glanced at Bruce. "Do you ever get texts that arrive in the voice of a disappointed adult?"

"Frequently."

"Business partner?"

"Butler."

Tony waited for elaboration. Bruce gave none.

"You have a butler. Of course you have a butler. It's like meeting an endangered species with a trust fund."

"He'd find that funny."

"Would he?"

"He'd say it more elegantly."

They might have continued indefinitely, two wealthy men using dry remarks to measure the other's attention, if the departure board had not changed.

The screen above Gate C19 went black, brightened, then displayed a single line in amber:

**C0 — PRIORITY TRANSFER — BOARDING**

No airline. No destination. No time.

A man standing beneath it pointed. "Where's C zero?"

Bell glanced up. Her lips parted.

"There is no C zero," she said.

The line vanished. Normal delays returned.

Tony's smile slipped, just enough for Bruce to see.

"System test?" Tony asked.

"Not one we've been told about," Bell said. She pushed back from her station. "I need to speak to operations."

Bruce set his coffee down. "When did you last see that display reset?"

"An hour ago, after the weather alert."

"Was it like this?"

"No."

Tony looked at Bruce. "Don't tell me. You find broken printers romantic and you've been waiting your whole life for a mystery in an airport."

Bruce rose. The storm threw light and shadow over his face.

"I was going to ask," he said, "whether you noticed the message came up on every monitor, not just this one."

Tony had noticed. The distant departures wall, the gate podium, even a tablet held by an airline employee seventy feet away had flashed amber in the same moment. His memory replayed it at once, as clear as video.

"I noticed," he said.

"And?"

"And those screens don't all belong to the same system."

Bruce picked up his bag.

For the first time since they had met, neither man had a joke ready.

Outside, thunder rolled beyond the runway. Somewhere deeper in the building, a door began to sound an alarm.
EOF

# Chapter Two: Weather Minimums

Bruce had been told, many times and by a variety of people, that airports were places of freedom.

He had never found them particularly convincing in that role.

They were places where movement was granted in small permissions. Take off your shoes. Show your identification. Stand behind the line. Wait to be called. Wait again. For most people the concessions were trivial, necessary, forgotten as soon as a plane lifted into the clouds. For Bruce, who spent much of his life studying the differences between safety and submission, the choreography was impossible to ignore.

Bellwether International was very good at choreography.

Its terminal had been rebuilt seven years earlier: a generous glass roof, trees under artificial lights, signs in four languages, a floor patterned to guide the eye toward security and shops. Little islands of charging stations gave the crowds a purpose while they waited. The art on the wall was abstract enough not to offend anyone. The food prices were offensive enough not to require art.

And now the terminal was full of stranded people.

Bruce watched the subtle changes. At first, passengers merely changed seats. Then they started to construct territories: rolling bags against chair legs, coats across adjacent places, families settling beside outlets. A line formed at the information desk, although everyone in it possessed the same information as the agent. The public wanted a person to hear their uncertainty and hand it back in a more useful shape.

Tony Stark, apparently, wanted to dismantle the problem with his teeth.

He had pursued Anika Bell as far as the service corridor before Bruce caught up.

"She's going to have security remove you," Bruce said.

"Security can't remove the weather. They could try starting small."

"Let her work."

"I'm helping."

"You're following."

"It's a subset of helping."

Bruce moved past him and stopped at a stainless-steel door marked AUTHORIZED STAFF ONLY. A small yellow light pulsed above it. Beneath the door he could see water flowing in a narrow line toward a drain.

"The door alarm," he said. "That one?"

Tony checked the pulse with his eyes. "Possibly. Pressure sensor or latch. Probably both."

"Would it be normal to have water there?"

"Depends whether we want the floor washed."

"We don't."

Tony looked down. Water on the polished tile caught reflections from the ceiling. A thin film, no more, but traveling from the wrong side of the door.

"Okay," he said. "That's less charming."

An airport employee in a navy rain jacket approached them with an expression that had been sharpened by a thousand urgent problems. Her radio was clipped at her shoulder, and she carried a clipboard thick enough to damage a foot if dropped.

"Gentlemen. This corridor is closed."

"We hadn't gone through it," Bruce said.

"Yet," Tony added.

"Then we're having a good start. I'm Leila Moreno, airport operations director for the evening. Can I help you get back to your gate?"

Bruce glanced at the wet line. "You have water coming under a secured door."

She looked once, crouched, touched two fingers to the tile, and pressed her radio. "Jonah, I need somebody at the north service vestibule. Minor seepage, source unknown. Bring cones."

The radio answered with static and a voice: "Copy, north service. Give me five."

Only then did Moreno look carefully at Bruce and Tony.

"Oh," she said. "You're the expensive problems."

"I've been called worse," Tony said.

"Not by me yet."

Bruce felt a brief, unexpected relief. Competence had a tone; Leila Moreno possessed it.

"The display at C19 showed an unauthorized boarding message," he said. "C0, priority transfer. There isn't a gate by that number."

Moreno's expression changed, not to alarm, but to a particular concentration. "When?"

"About four minutes ago," Tony said. "It propagated across displays with different owners."

"How do you know?"

Tony opened his mouth.

Bruce answered first. "Because the same information appeared simultaneously on the large departures board, the airline podium display, and a staff tablet. Ms. Bell can confirm."

Moreno's gaze moved between them. "I'll ask."

"We've got all the time in the world," Tony said. "Except when we don't."

"Mr. Stark, are you suggesting my team doesn't know how to do its job?"

"No. I'm suggesting somebody else's software may be doing a different job than you think."

Moreno considered that a moment. "That's a useful distinction. Don't lose it."

She led them back into the main concourse, not because she had agreed to include them but because she wasn't about to leave two men of their notoriety standing by a service door during a weather emergency. Bruce understood the calculation and respected it.

Near the central atrium, the ceiling lights dimmed once and returned. A child cried at the noise of a rolling metal shutter. A woman offered him a biscuit. The child accepted the biscuit as if negotiations had concluded successfully.

Moreno paused beside an operations kiosk. A young woman with closely cropped hair and a bright green jacket stood behind it, her attention fixed on two screens.

"Asha," Moreno said. "Did we push a priority-transfer announcement?"

"No, ma'am." The young woman frowned. "It wouldn't go out that way. And that designation isn't in the passenger display library."

"Any test running?"

"Not from this desk. Harbor Meridian's VELA program had a scheduled rehearsal today, but they postponed it because of weather. At least that's what the calendar says."

Tony stepped closer. "VELA. The occupancy and shelter-routing pilot?"

"Yes."

"I read their presentation in a panel yesterday. Great slides. Reassuring colors. Sentences that taste like expensive air conditioning."

"I helped install the operator displays," Asha said, then bit her lip, as though she'd spoken out of turn.

"Good," Moreno said. "Then you'll know if something is wrong."

Asha looked at the scrolling panel. "I know what normal looks like. That isn't the same thing."

Moreno nodded. "Fair answer."

Bruce moved to the edge of the kiosk. The occupancy map showed green rectangles for zones of the airport and a single amber rectangle for a staff transit hallway. In the upper right corner a number labeled *TOTAL SHELTERED PERSONS* counted upward in small, precise increments. It climbed by three. Then dropped by seventeen.

"What did that mean?" he asked.

Asha followed his gaze. "It adjusted when a group changed zones. It should balance out."

"Should?"

She examined a second screen. "Yes, sir. It should."

Another announcement came over the public speakers. The airport had begun asking people to move away from exterior glass and toward designated interior seating. Severe wind gusts had exceeded the building's ordinary operating threshold. No evacuation was ordered. No departure time could be provided.

Moreno lifted her radio again. "Anika, start moving C19 customers to C17 and C18. Keep families together. We have interior seating open."

There was a pause. "Who is helping you?" Bell asked.

"At the moment?" Moreno looked at the two men. "A philanthropist and a liability."

"Which one's which?" Tony asked.

"Still collecting data."

Bruce almost smiled.

Anika's reply crackled in the radio. "I need two wheelchairs, an attendant for Mr. Haddad, and three more people to help carry hand luggage. Half these folks have been waiting since noon."

"I'll send what I can."

Moreno started to move. Bruce caught the edge of her attention.

"I can carry bags."

"Of course you can."

"I don't need access to your systems to do that."

"That's the first reassuring thing anybody's said to me tonight."

Tony looked offended. "And I don't get a job?"

Moreno pointed toward the concourse. "There's a folding sign marked WET FLOOR near that service vestibule. Make sure nobody walks past it."

He stared. "You're assigning Tony Stark to a wet-floor sign."

"Are you too important to keep someone from falling?"

Something in her voice made the challenge cleaner than an insult.

Tony took the sign. "No," he said. "Of course not."

As Moreno left, Bruce watched Stark position the yellow warning triangle at the corner. A man in a gray sweater started toward the wet tiles. Tony guided him around with an open hand and a brief, unshowy word. Then he stood beside the sign, irritated and oddly still.

Bruce knew that kind of stillness. It belonged to people who had suddenly been given a task simple enough to remove every excuse not to do it.

He started toward C19 to help with the passengers.

At the kiosk, Asha called out, "Ms. Moreno?"

Leila turned.

"The number is doing it again."

On the green occupancy map, the total fell by forty-three.

No doors had opened. No flight had departed. Bruce had watched the crowd through the central aisle, and no forty-three people had walked by.

Asha refreshed the count. The number remained.

"Where did they go?" she whispered.

Nobody answered.
EOF

# Chapter Three: A Number on a Screen

Tony disliked problems that insisted on presenting themselves as statistics.

Statistics were wonderfully useful until someone forgot they were about something. He had spent several years of his life trying to make machines predict danger, then several more trying to make them stop predicting human beings as collateral costs. It had left him suspicious of any interface that looked calm while the room behind it grew louder.

The VELA map was almost aggressively calm.

Asha Desai brought up a second view while Moreno spoke to building security. Under the occupancy totals were seven green bars and one amber. The green bars named the public concourses; the amber bar belonged to Annex C, a service-and-shelter extension attached to the older terminal. The screen reported ZERO in Annex C.

"Forty-three people don't just disappear from a building," Tony said.

"They can disappear from this number," Asha replied. "It combines inputs. Tickets, badge events, door counters, manual entries. It doesn't really know who anybody is. It knows what the systems are reporting."

"Good. That's an important difference."

Asha gave him a quick, measuring look. "You know the product?"

"I know the language people use when they promise the world that computers can substitute for witnesses."

"I didn't say that."

"No. You didn't. Which is why I'm still here."

Asha stood straighter. "I was hired to help maintain the display layer. My badge doesn't let me change the logic or look inside the contractor's service. I can see the interface and the alerts it gives us."

"Excellent boundaries. Especially if somebody respects them."

Moreno returned with two security officers and Anika Bell, who had a folded paper manifest in her hand and the posture of a woman approaching a committee to explain the word *no.*

"My count says C19 had eighty-six passengers and three airline staff before the relocation," Bell said. "Sixty-eight passengers moved to the interior gates. Twelve went to the food court to find relatives. Six stayed behind because they wanted the windows. They were encouraged to move. Nobody signed out of existence."

Moreno placed the paper on the counter. "Could the forty-three be split across other areas?"

Asha scrolled. "I can look for zone changes, but the allocation view says the total fell. That's system-wide, not just local."

Bell folded her arms. "I have a passenger on oxygen, a family with a baby, and a nine-year-old asking me whether the airport is being punished for something. I don't have the luxury of worrying about your computer's feelings."

"Neither do I," Moreno said. "I need counts from each gate team, on paper. If the VELA map is wrong, the people are still here. We prepare for the people who are here."

A security officer glanced at Tony. "We don't need outsiders in the command conversation."

"I'm wearing a visitor badge," Tony said. "That's as outside as I can get."

Bruce, arriving with two rolling bags in each hand, paused just beyond the group. "Then help as an outsider."

"I am. I'm offering commentary."

"You're offering commentary on people doing the work."

Tony's reply rose to his tongue and stopped. Behind Bruce walked a small girl with a blue backpack and a woman in a maroon sweater. The girl stared openly at Tony.

"Are you Iron Man?" she asked.

"Depends. Are you my accountant?"

She did not laugh. She examined him with an unnerving seriousness.

"My dad says you made a suit that can fly in any weather."

"Your dad is optimistic. What's your name?"

"Nina."

"Hi, Nina. Who's your co-pilot?"

"Aunt Celia. My mom's in Denver. We were going to see her after her surgery."

Tony felt the familiar tug in his chest, the way a casual sentence could suddenly possess a weight that the speaker had no intention of sharing. A kid on one side of a country. A mother on the other. Flights reduced to numbers and weather symbols.

"Well," he said, lowering his voice, "we're going to make sure you both get what you need while we're stuck. Not just what the giant television says you need. Deal?"

Nina considered. "Do you have snacks?"

"I have a personal relationship with several snack manufacturers."

"That isn't the same thing."

Behind him, Bruce made a sound that might have been a cough.

Tony pointed. "The quiet guy has bags. The loud guy has a plan."

"The quiet guy already found her aunt," Bruce said.

"Show-off."

Celia thanked them and followed Bell toward the interior seating. Nina looked back once. "Iron Man?"

"Yeah?"

"Don't break the airport."

Tony placed a hand over his heart. "That is hurtful but, historically, a fair request."

She smiled at last.

When they had gone, Moreno looked at the printout in Bell's hand. "We have to account for everyone."

"Then let's count humans," Bruce said. "Not interfaces."

"That's what we're doing."

"I'll help," Tony said.

Moreno regarded him. "Both of you can help by following instructions. No unauthorized systems, no private security teams coming through my doors, and absolutely no public statements."

"My public statements are excellent."

"They're also loud."

Bruce said, "Agreed."

Tony turned. "You agree awfully quickly for a man whose hobby is doing things his own way."

"I didn't say I was good at following my own advice."

Moreno made an impatient gesture and sent them to assist with seating counts.

For the next forty-five minutes, they did the least glamorous work of their adult lives. Bruce walked rows, asking whether each traveler was with a group, recording names only when the passenger chose to share them. Tony distributed bottled water, carried a cart of blankets, and helped a gate agent improvise a legible numbering system for those who needed medical attention, translation, or assistance with movement. He spoke Spanish badly and understood more than he spoke. Bruce spoke it well enough that a woman whose husband had wandered to the wrong concourse looked at him with frank relief.

The terminal changed with the weather. Rain snapped against the windows in long white streaks. Metal under the roof creaked. Somewhere a door thudded again and again against its stop until maintenance secured it. The overhead announcements became shorter and more repetitive. Each time they said *temporary* ground stop, the word sounded less temporary.

At C17, a man named Malik Haddad sat with a small oxygen concentrator at his feet and a purse-sized battery pack in his lap. He was seventy-two, precise about his medication schedule, and annoyed that his daughter had insisted he travel with an extra charging cable.

"She thinks I am a toddler," he told Tony.

"That's what family does. They love you so much they become unbearable."

"You have family?"

"Several, by flexible definitions."

Malik smiled. "Then you understand."

Tony checked the power indicator, leaving the medical equipment itself alone. "We'll get you an outlet that stays powered, and an actual clinician to check on you. I can summon someone with qualifications exceeding mine."

"I hope so. Mine are considerable."

"Now that I believe."

A short time later Dr. Nia Okafor, an emergency physician traveling home from a conference, volunteered to organize the improvised aid desk with airport first responders. She was clear about the difference between helping and commandeering medical operations. Tony liked that, too, although he worried about discovering he liked all the people whose first response to his name was to ignore it.

At 21:11, the next roll call brought the count up by thirty-eight, then forty. The gap refused to close. A cluster of names on the paper ledger had no corresponding occupancy allocation. Asha marked them with a pencil.

"These are transfers from Terminal A and a hotel shuttle," she said. "They were rerouted earlier because of the wind. We don't have a clean arrival event for them in VELA."

"So the screen's missing people because the system never saw them come in," Tony said.

"Some, yes. But that's not all of them."

She showed them a row of names with a handwritten annotation: *C ANNEX / SHELTER ROUTE / 20:27.*

"Who sent them there?" Bruce asked.

"A shelter marshal. The weather alert indicated the interior gallery was safe."

"Where is it?"

Asha pointed beyond a bank of shuttered shops. "Old part of the building. Nice space, actually. Used to be the observation hall. It's connected to the service wing."

Tony studied the green rectangle marked ANNEX C — VACANT.

"And the system thinks there are zero people in it?"

"Yes."

"Did anybody physically check?"

Asha went still.

Moreno was already reaching for her radio.

"Jonah," she said, "I need eyes in Annex C. Tell me exactly what you see."

Static. Then a voice, rough with age and disbelief.

"Leila? I was about to call you. The inner shutters are down. Somebody's posted CLOSED — CLEARED on the door. My crew didn't do that."

"Can you get inside?"

"Not from where I'm standing."

Moreno looked at the map. "How long since you saw somebody in there?"

A pause.

"Forty minutes. Maybe more."

On the counter, the total sheltered count decreased by another three.

Tony stared at it. Numbers were still falling while everyone they could see stayed precisely where they were.

"Okay," he said quietly. "Now I dislike the interface."

# Chapter Four: C Zero

By nine forty-seven, Bruce had developed an inventory of Bellwether International's unpleasant noises.

There was the thin, familiar whine of wheels over tile. There was the heavier rattling of food carts pushed too fast. The ventilation system made a low oceanic rumble through the roof, interrupted every few minutes by a cough of moving dampers. When the storm pressed against the building, the glass answered with a sound like someone rubbing the rim of a large bowl.

And there was a new noise: a three-tone chime, identical to a gate call, followed by silence.

Bruce heard it first outside a closed gift shop. A dozen people looked up. The monitor above the shop blinked, displayed the amber message, and went blank before anyone else had finished reading it.

**C0 — PRIORITY TRANSFER — BOARDING**

The message had come from the same unfamiliar source as before. Bruce photographed it with his phone. The photograph showed the sign, a reflection of the crowd, and the minute hand of the nearby wall clock.

Tony joined him, pushing a cart full of bottled water like a disgruntled hotel employee.

"Either the universe is trying to board a flight that doesn't exist," Tony said, "or somebody has put a strange advertisement on a screen that doesn't sell advertising."

"The chime came before the text."

"So?"

"Someone wanted attention before they supplied information."

Tony squinted at the screen. "We're going to have to compare notes. Mine say the message is inappropriate. Yours say it's rude."

Bruce returned his phone to his pocket. "A real boarding call is attached to a person, an aircraft, a destination, and a procedure. This is attached to none of them."

"You think it's meant to move people."

"I think it's meant to make somebody look for a place."

Tony glanced toward the shuttered shops. "You're very cheerful at parties, aren't you?"

"I don't go to many."

"That I do not believe. The photographs are definitive."

"Those are usually fundraisers."

"Mine too. I spend most of mine wondering whether I can make the dessert table autonomous."

Bruce walked toward a floor directory. The map had no C0. It listed C1 to C42, with Annex C represented by a bland block labeled STAFF ACCESS / SHELTER. At the bottom of the directory, in slightly different typography, was a QR code for a live wayfinding service.

"Did you scan that?" Tony asked.

"Not yet."

"I can't believe Bruce Wayne fears QR codes."

"I don't fear them. I don't trust unexamined instructions."

"A distinction you might embroider on a pillow."

Bruce scanned it using a disposable browser session. The route service opened to a bright map with arrows guiding passengers toward designated shelter zones. One zone, the old observation gallery, showed green. When Bruce selected it, the route drew a cheerful blue path through the service corridor behind the gift shop.

"What do you see?" Tony asked.

"A passenger route through a staff corridor."

"That's not normal."

"Doesn't mean it's wrong during an emergency. But we need to know who approved it."

Tony studied the map over his shoulder. "It's the same VELA branding."

At the center of the screen, a small line read *Occupancy-verified safety routing.* Bruce enlarged it.

"A promise," he said. "We should find out what it was based on."

They returned to Moreno, who had set up an operations station in an empty conference suite near C14. The room smelled of carpet cleaner and the stale warmth of projector bulbs. Folding tables supported laptops, radios, paper maps, handwritten counts, and an improbably neat pile of granola bars. Nobody had enough spare attention to be impressed by the fact that Bruce Wayne and Tony Stark were standing in the doorway.

A broad-shouldered man in gray workwear was speaking quietly to Moreno. He had a silver beard, deeply lined hands, and a flashlight hanging from his belt.

"I checked the east side," he said. "The rolling shutters are down, but they didn't come down in sequence with the storm doors. There's a printed notice on the inside. Somebody posted it after the transfer."

"Can you reach the old west stairs?" Moreno asked.

"Not with the water level in that passage. It's a sump problem, not a flood, but I'm not sending anybody through it until we know what's live."

"Good."

Bruce could feel Tony watching the exchange. Stark was quick, ingenious, used to being the person who knew the most in a room. Here the older maintenance man understood systems the way a person understands a body he has lived in for forty years. It was not an insult to Tony. It was a reminder of scale.

Moreno saw the two men. "Please tell me you haven't found a second mysterious door."

"We found a route telling people to use the first," Bruce said. He showed her the display.

She read it, then asked the maintenance man, "Jonah, did this passage ever carry passengers?"

"Not for twenty years. Fire code's allowed shelter occupancy in the gallery, sure, but the transfer map's supposed to take them through the public bridge. That's where staff and wheelchairs can go."

"The live map does not," Bruce said.

"Then the live map is wrong," Jonah Price answered.

It was said without surprise, only an exhausted emphasis on the ordinary possibility of error.

"Get me the current printed plan," Moreno said. "Asha, call the contractor's on-site representative. Tell them we need an explanation for the C0 announcement and every route to Annex C."

Asha made a note. "Cass Finch is still in the airport. I saw her at the resilience symposium."

Tony's eyes narrowed. "The VELA woman? Red glasses, navy suit, says 'predictive assurance' like it's a food group?"

"That's her," Asha said.

"She's a vice president at Harbor Meridian," Tony explained to Bruce. "Their risk operations arm. She gave a talk about turning messy crisis response into measurable workflows. Every third slide had a photograph of a smiling family."

"Was her presentation wrong?" Bruce asked.

"It was beautiful," Tony said. "That's not an answer."

At the far end of the room, Bell entered with Celia and Nina. Nina's blue backpack was still on her shoulders. She clutched a folded boarding pass as if it were a ticket to somewhere more certain.

"Sorry to interrupt," Bell said. "This one's got a story."

Nina looked at the adults. "It's not a story. I saw the people."

Moreno crouched so their eyes were level. "Who did you see?"

"The lady in the orange coat took a bunch of people down there." Nina pointed toward the old shops. "She had a sign with a moon on it. They were supposed to be safe because the windows were small. My aunt said we should stay with our airplane people."

"Did you hear her name?"

"No. She said *priority group*, and it made the older man angry because he said he'd been priority for three hours already."

Tony stifled a grin. "I like him."

Nina looked at him. "Then she had everyone stand in a line. Forty, I think. Maybe fifty. They took the lady with the green scarf in a chair. She dropped her hat."

"Did you see where they went?" Bruce asked.

"Through the shiny doors. The doors near the statue with no head."

Bruce turned to the airport map. The sculpture outside the closed gift shop was a modernist aluminum figure with an abstract oval for a head; from a child's height, it could easily be perceived as headless. Nina had described an exact landmark. That mattered.

"Were you afraid?" Moreno asked.

Nina shook her head. "It looked organized."

After Nina and Celia left, the room was quiet in a new way.

"Forty-three," Tony said. "She's describing forty-something people, and our computer loses forty-three."

"Don't convert her estimate into proof," Bruce said. "We need the actual list."

"I know that."

"Then say it correctly."

Tony's smile thinned. "Fine. We have a lead."

Jonah unfolded an old paper plan on the table. The observation gallery was an elongated trapezoid along the north side of Terminal C, separated from the service corridor by a line of rolling shutters and marked by two exits. One led back to the main terminal. The other led to an enclosed walkway over a low-lying equipment yard.

"There's room for sixty people if the seating is cleared," Jonah said. "More if you're just standing. But this was never meant to be the main shelter. The ventilation's on a separate circuit."

"Can we contact anyone in there?" Moreno asked.

"An old intercom. If it still works."

Asha tried the number. There was a burst of static, a click, and a voice.

"Gallery station."

Everyone leaned toward the speaker.

The voice belonged to an older man. It sounded frightened, not because he was shouting but because he was trying so carefully not to.

"This is airport operations," Moreno said. "Who am I speaking to?"

"Frankie Saye. Custodial team. I'm here with a few people. Is someone coming?"

"Yes, Frankie. How many people do you have?"

"I don't know exactly. I counted forty-three at the start, but the group got separated when the shutters came down. Some of them are in the other half. A woman has trouble breathing. Someone says the corridor on that side is getting wet."

Moreno closed her eyes for half a second. "Are you safe where you are?"

"Not sure. They told us we were in the safest part of the airport."

A small electronic chime sounded over the intercom. C zero, three notes, then silence.

Frankie whispered, "Oh no. Not again."

The connection died.
EOF

# Chapter Five: The Room That Was Empty

The first question Tony asked was not why the intercom had failed.

It was why nobody had called Frankie Saye before the two celebrities showed up.

Moreno answered it without defensiveness. "Because the system reported Annex C empty, and the shelter marshal who escorted the group had not checked back in. The paper roster was still being consolidated. That is what happened. It's not what should have happened."

"Can I hear that last sentence again?" Tony asked.

"Which part?"

"The part where a person in authority took responsibility in fewer than four words. I want to frame it."

She gave him a look over the edge of her radio. "Get a frame after the emergency."

Asha checked the intercom line with facilities. The endpoint was still powered, but calls were not connecting. Jonah Price had a crew at the old public bridge. He could get to the shutter housing, but the emergency control panel reported a fault, and the local procedure required a second staff member and a verified clearance before the shutters could be raised. The corridor adjacent to the old west stairs had standing water. He was not willing to send anyone into it until his electricians declared the area safe.

Tony had the urge to say he could solve all of those problems by tomorrow morning with six engineers and a modest budget. Unfortunately, tomorrow morning was not the problem.

"What is the fallback?" Bruce asked.

Moreno pointed to the paper plan. "Open the public bridge under facilities control, verify fire compartment status, send responders in with communications, and account for everyone. We do not improvise around a shutter with people behind it."

Tony gestured toward the room. "There. Excellent. A plan with verbs."

The door opened. A woman entered carrying a hard-sided computer case. She was perhaps forty-five, with dark red glasses and a navy suit beneath a clear airport poncho. She had the composure of an executive who had just been awakened on a Sunday morning and decided her staff must never know it.

"Leila," she said. "I heard you needed our team."

"Cass. I need your VELA duty log, the identity of whoever approved routing to Annex C, and an explanation for why forty-three people in that annex are listed as zero."

Cass Finch set her case on an empty chair. "I'm going to need to understand the scope before I promise you a root cause."

Tony let out a small, theatrical breath. "That's what they all say right before the slide deck comes out."

Finch looked at him without surprise. "Mr. Stark. I'm sorry you're stranded."

"I have been called worse by better systems."

"I didn't design the weather."

"Good, because there'd be a recall."

Finch turned back to Moreno. "The shelter census excludes passengers who have been transferred into protected manual-management status. That data is visible in the incident layer rather than the public occupancy display. We were advised to remove duplicate counts from the primary summary."

"Who advised you?" Bruce asked.

Finch's gaze moved to him. "Our airport integration committee. The safety logic underwent review."

"Who signed the approval?"

"I'd have to retrieve it."

Bruce inclined his head. "Please do."

Tony watched Finch process that. Questions asked by people who could afford to hire expensive lawyers had a different texture, but Bruce's question was not a threat. It was an invitation to produce evidence. Finch appeared to find it more inconvenient than a threat.

Asha brought up the screen. "If they're excluded from the primary summary, where does the dispatcher see them?"

"There is a protected management view," Finch said.

"I don't have access to it."

"Your station is an operator console. That is an incident-command function."

Moreno raised a hand. "I am incident command. Show me."

Finch opened her case. There was an awkward minute of wireless negotiation, screen permissions, and the kind of murmured assistance that made Tony's eye twitch. Eventually a second display appeared. Annex C showed *GROUP TRANSFERRED — STATUS CLOSED*. Beneath it, a small note identified a class of exceptions.

"Closed?" Moreno said. "They're inside."

"Closed means the shelter-relocation transaction completed," Finch explained. "Not that each person departed the airport."

"Then your dashboard calls an unfinished job finished," Bruce said.

"It calls the transaction finished. The distinction is in the documentation."

Bell, standing near the doorway, let out an incredulous laugh. "You should come tell the people who are trapped what the documentation thinks."

Finch's face tightened. "I understand why this seems upsetting."

"Seems?" Bell asked.

Moreno intervened. "Our problem is not how it seems. Our problem is that a staff member is asking us to come and get forty-three people we were told had been accounted for. Cass, I need the names, the zone access instructions, and a way to make sure no other group can disappear from my count."

Finch took a breath and nodded. "You can expect cooperation."

Tony had known hundreds of people who made that promise. There were the ones who meant it and the ones who meant that they would cooperate after determining what cooperation would cost them. Finch's expression was not quite either. It was the face of someone who had already calculated a cost and discovered that the sum was moving.

Asha printed the incident summary. The old printer coughed once and produced a page of gray horizontal bands.

"Oh, for—" Tony began.

Bell moved between him and the printer as if guarding a museum exhibit. "Don't."

"I could buy the whole manufacturer."

"Then send better printers. This one is currently on duty."

He held up both hands.

Asha rerouted the job to a second printer in the office. The page emerged with a tiny footer at the bottom: *VELA Demonstration Environment — Harbor Meridian*. She read it twice.

"What is this?" she asked Finch.

"A legacy name for the pilot instance."

"This was deployed to production three weeks ago. Why is it still labeled demonstration?"

Finch glanced at the page. "Because the branding migration hasn't been completed."

"But the data is live?"

"Of course."

"And the exception categories?"

"Tested."

Tony leaned forward. "In what environment?"

"Mr. Stark—"

"I'm curious. It's a harmless condition. Usually. If your demonstration environment is handling real emergency headcounts, and the 'protected' group disappears from the main occupancy total, I'd like to know what was tested."

Finch's voice flattened. "Our system meets its contract requirements."

"That wasn't the question."

Bruce stood still beside the table. He looked at Finch's hands instead of her face. She kept one palm over the hard case, almost unconsciously, as if protecting the machine from a fall.

"We can examine the contract later," he said. "Right now, will the software interfere with opening the bridge?"

"The building systems are separate," Finch said. "VELA provides advisory routing."

"Then it cannot command the shutters."

"No."

"Good," Bruce said. "That narrows our problem."

It also narrowed Finch's room to maneuver. Tony saw that too.

In the hallway a luggage cart rattled by, wheels shrieking against tile. Somewhere in the concourse a passenger began singing quietly to a child. The ordinary sounds kept coming, indifferent to the fact that a computer had declared forty-three human beings completed.

Moreno called the facilities desk. Jonah was ready at the public bridge. The safety clearance would take another half hour. She requested a second response team, then spoke into her radio with a firmness that admitted no argument.

"Annex C is occupied. Repeat, Annex C is occupied. Until every person is located, nobody closes that incident."

The phrase moved through the room like a window opening.

Tony looked at the screen again. Forty-three remained a zero in green.

He found that he hated the color more than the number.

# Chapter Six: A Paper Cup of Bad Coffee

The coffee in Bellwether International's operations suite had the flavor of a punishment that had been issued several shifts earlier and left standing.

Bruce drank it anyway.

Leila Moreno stood at the table where her team had assembled the latest roll calls. They had written gate numbers on folded sheets of paper and arranged them around a map. Nobody had requested a billionaire's opinion on the layout. For the moment, Bruce was content with that.

It had been a long time since he had been allowed to be merely useful.

He put a cup beside Moreno without interrupting her. She looked at it, then at him.

"You didn't ask how I take it."

"Black."

"How did you know?"

"You've had three cups. No cream, no sugar."

"You watch everybody this closely?"

"Not everybody."

"That's the least reassuring answer you could have given."

He sat on the edge of an unused chair. Rain beat against the dark windows. A clock on the far wall read 22:34. On the radio, Jonah's crew discussed safety checks in clipped, patient voices. Nothing moved faster because the group at the table wanted it to.

"You came from the ramp," Bruce said.

Moreno turned. "What gives that away?"

"You don't look at the status monitor when the radio calls about a vehicle. You look toward the door. You still expect to hear the engine."

"You a detective on your days off?"

"Something like that."

"I loaded cargo at seventeen. Worked my way into airfield operations. They handed me a desk and a title when my knees started complaining. The knees are right, but they don't run this place."

She took the coffee. "And you? People tell me you're very good at signing checks."

"I learned early that signing a check is not the same as fixing what made it necessary."

"Then you've learned more than half the donors who tour this airport."

Bruce looked down at the paper map. There were handwritten names in the corners, notes about medication and language, markers for wheelchairs, infants, and people unable to stand in a long line. The marks did not form a beautiful dashboard. They formed a messy, legible record of a human situation.

"Why did you keep the paper system?" he asked.

"Because the last time the phones went down, somebody told us we'd lost a hundred and eighty passengers. We hadn't. We'd lost the ability to see them on a screen. The boarding agents still knew their names."

"When was that?"

"Four years ago. Lightning took the network for forty minutes. We resolved it with clipboards. Then a vendor flew in and told us the problem was that we hadn't invested enough in software."

Bruce felt something close to shame, not because he had built that vendor's software but because Wayne Foundation grants had helped airports across the country modernize systems with exactly that vocabulary. Transparent. Seamless. Frictionless. Often they meant harmless things: accessible signs, translated directions, easier evacuation notices. Sometimes they meant removing the last person authorized to ask whether the machine was wrong.

He thought of his father, who had believed a city could be repaired by building the right things in the right places. He had not been foolish to believe it. But the city had changed after the buildings were built. People always did.

Moreno turned a page. "You look like you're about to tell me how much this department needs a grant."

"No."

"Good. I don't have time for a speech."

"I was thinking I should find out what you've been asking for and not getting."

She looked at him properly then.

"Two more backup radios. Better training hours. An honest inspection of the north gallery, which was supposed to happen in September and got postponed because the contractor wanted to finish the pilot."

"Who postponed it?"

"A deputy commissioner signed the schedule change. We fought it. You can read the minutes later."

"I will."

Moreno's eyes narrowed. "That sounded expensive."

"Most of my bad habits are."

"Try not to make this one a surprise."

From the doorway Tony said, "There you are. I was beginning to think you'd joined a monastery with a very strict dress code."

He came in carrying two paper bags from the sole open restaurant. On his face was the careful casualness Bruce associated with people who had been working too long and didn't want to acknowledge they were tired.

"What did you buy?" Moreno asked.

"Everything they had that could survive seventeen minutes without refrigeration. Sandwiches, fruit, bread, things pretending to be sandwiches, and something the cashier called a savory bun. My legal team may be involved."

"Did you pay?"

"I'm wounded."

"Did you pay the posted price?"

Tony hesitated. "I tipped."

"How much?"

"Enough that she's threatening to shut the till early."

Moreno sighed. "People still need the counter. Don't turn the staff into a story."

Tony's expression sobered. "Okay. You're right. I'll settle the food for this room and leave the counter alone."

Bruce caught the exchange. Stark's generosity was impulsive, almost kinetic. He saw a need and wanted it gone by force of will, money, or machinery. Bruce's habit was different, but not always better. He studied the need until he'd designed a system for it, sometimes while the need sat beside him wondering when anyone would bring dinner.

Tony slid a sandwich onto Bruce's map.

"Eat."

"I'm working."

"And I'm intensely irritating, yet I still eat."

"Frequently."

"There he is."

They sat opposite each other while Moreno took a call. The first bite of the sandwich was dry. The second was better. Bruce hadn't realized how hungry he was.

"You're not what I expected," Tony said.

"Neither are you."

"Liar. I'm exactly what every headline promises."

"That's what you want them to think."

Tony lifted the paper cup. "Wow. We are skipping the shallow end."

"You talk so people won't ask you questions."

"And you stop talking so they won't know your answers."

Bruce considered that. "Fair."

Tony looked out through the conference room's glass wall, where airport staff hurried past with clipboards and blankets. Asha stood at the kiosk, showing a colleague how to record manual counts without losing the time stamps. Anika Bell argued with a man who wanted to know why the airline had not arranged a replacement aircraft to fly into the weather. The man seemed unable to imagine that a replacement aircraft would encounter the same air.

"Pepper keeps telling me to let people do their jobs," Tony said. "She says there are only two kinds of people who never delegate: idiots and tyrants."

"Which does she think you are?"

"Depends whether I've slept."

Bruce ate another bite.

"Alfred says I don't trust other people to finish anything," he said. "Sometimes he's right."

"And sometimes?"

"Sometimes I have evidence."

Tony grinned. "We could build an entire friendship on that sentence."

Moreno's radio crackled before Bruce could respond.

"Leila, Jonah. We have a problem at the bridge. The approved plan identifies a public entrance. But the contractor's emergency placards mark it as a restricted egress. They can't both be right, and I need written authorization before I open it with civilians on the other side."

Moreno closed her eyes for a beat. "Who has the current plan?"

"Facilities archive. Printed and signed. I can send a runner."

"Do it. No shortcuts."

Tony stood immediately. "Let me go."

"You're not staff," Moreno said.

"I have legs. They came standard."

"You'll go with an escort and stay where you're told."

Bruce got up too.

"Both of you?" Moreno asked.

"He'll get distracted and buy the archives," Bruce said.

Tony pointed at him. "I like you less when you make accurate predictions."

Moreno handed them a paper requisition and gave the runner's name. "This isn't a treasure hunt. Bring me the signed plan, not a theory."

Bruce took the paper.

They went to look for an answer that would fit in an envelope.
EOF

# Chapter Seven: The People in the Margin

The concourse had become a small provisional city.

People who would never willingly speak to one another during an ordinary flight delay had begun exchanging names, phone chargers, ibuprofen, and advice. A chef stranded between shifts organized a warm-food queue. A retired school principal found six children with no interest in sleeping and set up a game in which they mapped the terminal using only the things they could see from their chairs. A woman in a red sweater translated announcements into Arabic and French for her neighbors. When she tired, a college student took over.

Tony moved through it with the paper requisition in his hand, accompanied by a security escort named Lyle, and found himself in a peculiar position: almost nobody cared who he was.

This wasn't entirely true. Several people took photographs. One man requested a selfie at a moment when Tony was trying to balance a folding chair over a puddle. But the prevailing mood was not celebrity excitement. People were busy. They had settled into the old human business of enduring an event together, and celebrity was a decorative extra.

At the aid station Nia Okafor was examining Malik Haddad's portable concentrator while a first responder checked the equipment supply list. She waved Tony over.

"You said you'd find a stable place to charge this?"

"Leila's staff assigned one near the interior wall. I checked with the electrical team; the outlet is on the supported public area. No creative wiring by yours truly."

"Good. And don't touch his settings."

"I would never."

"I've met your type before."

"Billionaires?"

"Men who think a complex medical device is a challenge to their competence."

Tony glanced at Malik. "She's very strict."

"We are both lucky she is," Malik said.

Okafor gave him a smile. "I am strict because Mr. Haddad isn't a project. He is a person who needs to breathe comfortably until we can get him where he's going."

"I know," Tony said, too fast.

She tilted her head. "Then slow down long enough to show it."

He opened his mouth. Nothing came out. It was an unfamiliar experience and, he had to admit, informative.

A small boy nearby made a paper airplane from a discarded boarding stub. It flew for three feet, struck the base of a charging station, and fell. He retrieved it, studied the crease, and tried again. The second attempt traveled farther.

Tony watched him without commentary.

"You okay?" Okafor asked.

"Fine."

"That's not a particularly convincing report."

"Been told."

She lowered her voice. "I wasn't asking you to stop helping. I was asking you to notice what kind of help is being requested."

He nodded. This time it was real.

"The man in Annex C," Tony said. "Frankie. Did you hear the call?"

"I heard there are people in an inaccessible shelter. They may have one woman in respiratory distress. Operations is arranging access. If that changes, I need information immediately."

"I'll make sure it reaches you."

Okafor stepped away to speak to the responders. Tony looked toward the thin row of cots set up against an interior wall. An old man slept with his cap over his eyes. A mother bounced a baby. A teenager sat near the window reading a paperback so carefully that Tony could almost see the effort it took to stop watching the storm.

A paper tag hung from every cot. Not a medical record, not an identity badge. A way to keep track of who needed what.

It was embarrassingly simple.

Bruce returned from the corridor with a stack of printed floor plans and a young airport employee who had retrieved them from records. The runner, Emil, handed the plans to Lyle for transfer to Moreno.

"Signed in September?" Tony asked.

"August," Bruce said. "The revision in September was never approved."

"That sounds like a bureaucratic difference."

"It could be an important one."

They stopped beside a display of model aircraft, remnants of a museum exhibit from the old terminal. A polished silver biplane hung above their heads. Its wings had been fashioned with such delicacy that it looked more trustworthy than the systems surrounding it.

Bruce spread the plans on a nearby table. "See the gallery? The August plan has two public approaches. The September overlay removes one and converts the other to staff access. It also marks the area as a temporary shelter. But there is no signature authorizing both changes together."

"Meaning they changed the route but nobody signed off on the people who'd be using it."

"Meaning the route and the shelter designation may have been updated under different procedures. We don't know what someone approved."

Tony looked at him. "You do love precision."

"It keeps us from blaming the wrong person."

"And occasionally from discovering the right one before breakfast."

Bruce rolled the pages. "If you want to accuse someone, first learn what happened."

Tony felt an unreasonable flicker of anger. He did know what was happening. He could see the shape of the failure: elegant software, poorly tested assumptions, executives who were too impressed by their own diagrams, people forgotten in an old part of a building. He had watched versions of it happen in factories, hospitals, and cities. Each time the people in charge had insisted their intentions were good until the evidence became expensive.

But Bruce was right about the accusation. And that was worse than if he'd been wrong.

"Fine," Tony said. "We learn."

"Good."

"But I get to be insufferable after."

"I don't think that's dependent on permission."

Their escort gave a startled laugh. Tony looked at him.

"Lyle, you are going to have to choose a side."

"Sir, I have a shift that ends at six."

"Neutrality. The coward's refuge."

Bruce returned the plans to their envelope. "The intelligent man's shift management."

Lyle grinned.

On the way back they passed the shuttered corridor. A narrow strip of water still ran from the sealed service vestibule. It had spread enough that Jonah's crew had cordoned off a larger area. A woman in orange outerwear was taking photographs of the caution signs with a company-issued tablet.

Bruce noticed her first. He stopped.

"Excuse me," he said. "Are you with the shelter program?"

The woman looked up. She had close-set eyes, weather-reddened cheeks, and a lanyard bearing Harbor Meridian's logo.

"I'm a guest-relocation coordinator," she said. "Is something wrong?"

Tony glanced at the orange coat. "You might be able to tell us. Were you working this zone earlier?"

"I helped move people to the gallery. It was marked safe."

"Did you count them?" Bruce asked.

"We had a roster. Forty-three, I think. Why?"

"The gallery is now closed. Those people may need assistance."

The woman went pale. "Closed? We left them with the on-site attendant. VELA marked the transfer complete."

"Who told you it was complete?" Bruce asked.

She stared at him. "The tablet."

Tony looked at the orange lanyard, the camera, the rain on the glass, the paper plan in Bruce's hand.

"What's your name?" he asked gently.

"Imani Cole."

"Imani, we need you to come talk to Leila. And I think you should bring everything you remember from the transfer."

"Am I in trouble?"

Tony had been asked that question by technicians, pilots, soldiers, and children. It never became easier.

"I don't know," he said. "But we're going to make sure the truth gets there before an accusation does."

Bruce's gaze met his for one brief second.

It was the first decision that night on which they had agreed without an argument.

# Chapter Eight: An Audit of Small Things

When Bruce Wayne was twelve, Alfred had taught him how to clean a clock.

Not because the clock required a boy's assistance. The mantel clock had kept excellent time for seventy years and could doubtless have continued without him. Alfred had set the tools on a white cloth, removed the back, and shown Bruce how many small agreements were required for the hands to move.

"A complicated thing," Alfred had said, "is often very simple if you stop trying to understand it all at once."

Bruce remembered the lesson now in the operations suite, where an airport of steel, glass, and four hundred thousand daily transactions had been reduced to pages on a table.

The trouble with the pages was that each one told the truth about a different thing.

Anika Bell's boarding roster recorded eighty-six passengers at C19. Imani Cole's transfer sheet recorded forty-three people moved into Annex C, including two wheelchair users and one traveler who required the assistance of another person. Jonah Price's maintenance report recorded shutter faults beginning at 20:42, fifteen minutes after the transfer was marked complete. The VELA incident summary recorded Annex C as cleared at 20:31.

None of those documents, on its own, was a confession.

Together they made a timeline that did not fit.

"You said you left the group with an attendant," Bruce told Imani.

"Yes. Frankie. He was taking a supply cart to the gallery. He said he'd wait until somebody came to relieve him."

"Did someone take over?"

"I don't know. My next assignment was to guide travelers away from the east windows."

"Who gave you that assignment?"

"The contractor coordination channel. It looked routine. The app told me where I was needed."

Bruce nodded. "Did it tell you who was responsible for the group afterward?"

Imani stared at her hands. "No. It said the transfer was complete."

"If you hadn't seen that message, what would you have done?"

"Stayed until the airport marshal acknowledged the handoff. That's the way we practiced."

"Then the software changed the handoff."

"It did," she said. "I thought that was what they wanted."

Moreno made a note. "Who trained you?"

"Harbor Meridian ran a two-hour session and a refresher. We practiced on tablets in a conference room. All the examples were clean. The system never showed a transfer as complete unless the receiving person checked it."

Cass Finch, who had returned to the room with an engineer from her team, said, "The production service uses a different status vocabulary. Imani wouldn't have had to know the internal codes."

Bruce turned. "She did have to know whether she was permitted to leave people behind."

The answer came from the contractor engineer, not Finch. "The training materials should have covered that distinction."

Finch glanced at him. He fell silent.

"What's your name?" Moreno asked.

"Evan Lowe. Integration support."

"Evan, can you provide your training materials and the list of people who attended?"

"Yes."

"Do that. And don't let anyone edit them."

Lowe nodded too quickly.

Bruce filed the reaction away, not as proof but as a reason to ask more questions.

The room emptied gradually as staff went about their assigned work. Bruce remained with the paper roster and the clock on the wall. He had learned to distrust dramatic revelations. In real investigations, causes were often hiding inside the corrections nobody had bothered to make. A footnote. A signature. A date written by a clerk who had eaten a sandwich while working and wanted to go home.

He called Alfred from the hall. The signal connected on the third attempt.

"Master Bruce. I trust the trip has gone smoothly."

"Weather. I'm still in Port Mercer."

"I gathered as much from the news. I was considering dispatching a canoe."

"Roads are flooded."

"Then a very good canoe."

Bruce glanced toward the operations suite. "I may be here overnight."

"I have already told the house not to expect you. There is soup in the refrigerator for whenever you arrive."

For a moment, the offer struck him more sharply than it should have. An ordinary meal kept warm by someone who had been there his entire life. Bruce thought of Frankie Saye in a sealed room, a custodial employee left to care for forty-three strangers without anyone promising him supper.

"Alfred. Could you have someone quietly look into a company called Harbor Meridian? Public contracts, safety audits, litigation, anything available through ordinary channels. No interference with the local authorities."

"Naturally. Are you looking for a particular issue?"

"I don't know yet."

"You sound pleased."

"I sound tired."

"The two are not mutually exclusive in your case."

The call broke with a crackle before Bruce could answer. He stared at the screen, then slipped the phone away. The world outside the airport had become a place he could reach only in broken sentences.

When he returned to the suite, Tony was standing with Asha beside the second monitor. He had removed his jacket and rolled up his sleeves. It made him look younger, more like a man in a laboratory than the person who appeared on magazine covers.

"I think I found something," Tony said.

"You seem unsurprised," Bruce replied.

"I'm gifted with a healthy faith in my own curiosity."

Asha pointed to a printed annotation. "The routing pilot originally counted the annex as part of Terminal C. Then someone separated it into a manual exception zone. That's why the ordinary occupancy view doesn't include it."

"Was the change approved?" Bruce asked.

"The change request has a sign-off from Harbor Meridian. The airport field is blank on the copy I can see."

"Could there be another copy?"

"Yes."

Bruce took the page. "We'll ask for it."

Tony exhaled. "One day, I am going to take you to a casino. I want to see if you insist on having notarized receipts before placing a bet."

"You know what they say about people who gamble without understanding the odds."

"They have more fun?"

"They finance casinos."

From the far end of the hall came a metallic shudder. The building lights dipped, steadied, then changed to a softer backup level. The hum of the air system decreased until Bruce could hear the wind striking the windows individually.

Moreno's radio began issuing overlapping calls.

"Power transfer in Terminal C."

"We have a local ventilation fault."

"North gallery environmental circuit is no longer reporting."

Bruce looked at the paper roster. The date, the signature, the missing handoff. He had been assembling a clock one piece at a time, and now the hands were moving whether he understood it or not.

Tony was already halfway to the door.

"Wait," Bruce said.

"I'm not going to wait while forty-three people lose ventilation."

"Neither am I. But we need to know where to go."

For an instant Tony's temper showed nakedly. Then he took a breath.

"Fine," he said. "You find where. I'll make sure we're ready when you do."

He left at a run, this time toward the aid station.

Bruce found that he did not try to stop him.
EOF

# Chapter Nine: The Founder Problem

There were two things Tony Stark could do very well without looking.

One was solve a differential equation in his head. The other was recognize a corporate sentence that had been designed to keep its speaker from being sued.

Cass Finch had been using a great many of the second kind.

In the restaurant concourse, Tony stood beside a darkened espresso bar and listened to Pepper's voice on a call that kept losing one syllable in every seven.

"—not the vendor of record," she said. "We sold the component business years ago. It might be running Stark-derived infrastructure, but that doesn't make us the system integrator."

"I know how a supply chain works, Pep."

"Then stop talking as if you personally wrote the airport's software."

"I'm not."

"You said 'we built this' twice in the last minute."

Tony rubbed his forehead. Around him the terminal's emergency lights gave the ceiling a blue-gray gloom. The chairs under the espresso bar had been put upside down on the tables before the storm; the manager had not expected customers to sleep in his workplace.

"I'm looking at a product that borrows half the architecture people on my payroll invented," Tony said. "It gets sold as a safety platform, and when the safety part breaks, everyone points at the sales contract."

"Because the contract tells us who's accountable. We can help without stealing the investigation."

He knew that. The maddening thing about Pepper was that she could say what he already knew and make it sound like a discovery he had been trying to avoid.

"Send me the public procurement files and the current ownership chart. No privileged material. If we're tied to the project through licensing, I want the relationship documented."

"Already sent."

"Marry me again."

"Work on the airport first. Also, Tony?"

"Yes?"

"Eat something that isn't sugar."

The line went dead. He smiled at the phone, then checked the documents she had managed to send. Harbor Meridian was a composite company born from several acquisitions. Its VELA platform had an elegant name, a complicated sales structure, and a tendency to describe human procedural safeguards as "operator friction." Stark infrastructure appeared in its equipment stack, several generations removed from Tony's own engineering decisions. None of that proved wrongdoing. It was an uncomfortable footnote to an uncomfortable night.

Tony stared at the words *operator friction*.

He had once said something almost identical in an early design review. Not about an airport. About a production line. At the time he had meant preventable delay, human pain, wasted hours. He had assumed engineers would know the difference between a worker slowing a machine for a good reason and a worker slowing it because a button was badly placed.

He had not asked how the distinction would survive a sales meeting.

"You look like a man who just found his fingerprints on someone else's trouble," Bruce said.

Tony turned. Wayne stood in the aisle with his coat folded over one arm. His shirt sleeves were still neat. It was an act of personal hostility.

"You look like a man who irons his socks."

"Only the formal ones."

Tony held up the phone. "Harbor Meridian has licensed tech from one of my old companies. Not the routing rules, not the status definitions. Infrastructure. But enough to make me regret a few pleasant earnings calls."

Bruce said nothing.

"You can go ahead," Tony said. "This is your moment to tell me I built a monster."

"Did you?"

"No. I built a tool. Somebody else assembled this product."

"Then understand where your responsibility starts and stops."

"That's a more nuanced answer than I expected."

"I'm capable of nuance."

"That's also what an assassin would say."

Bruce's face did not change. "Interesting choice."

Tony laughed despite himself. "You know what I mean."

They walked toward the old museum display. Beneath the suspended silver biplane, two airport workers were distributing small battery-powered lights to families. Several passengers applauded them. The workers looked embarrassed and carried on.

"Does Wayne have any connection to this project?" Tony asked.

Bruce paused. "A charitable grant. Accessibility research, published route standards, consultation with disability advocates. Not the vendor. I approved the program summary last year."

"So the other billionaire has fingerprints too."

"Yes."

"Well, that's less lonely."

"It doesn't change what's happening."

"No. It doesn't."

They stood beneath the model aircraft. Its wings threw delicate shadows on the tiled floor.

"My father liked things that looked finished," Tony said. "The shape of a building, the shine on a machine. He could stand in a room he'd built and imagine he'd answered every question in it."

Bruce looked upward. "Mine liked things that made a city feel less alone. Hospitals. Trains. Shelters. He thought money could make room for people."

"Could it?"

"Sometimes."

"And the rest of the time?"

"It made room for people who were good at asking for it."

That landed with the clarity of a small hammer on glass.

Tony studied Bruce. There was an old grief in his face that cameras had never learned how to catch. It wasn't theatrical. It lived below the expression, as familiar as bone.

"You got the charitable version of the daddy issues," Tony said, almost gently.

"There's no charitable version."

For the first time Tony did not rush to contradict him.

Anika Bell approached, carrying two pages of handwriting and wearing a look of grim purpose.

"Mr. Wayne, Mr. Stark. Ms. Moreno needs you. The contractor's incident report says forty-three transfers were verified at twenty-thirty-one. Our handwritten sheet says the receiving attendant never checked them in. Imani Cole confirms it."

"Could be a mistake in the report," Tony said.

"Yes. It could," Bell replied. "But it's the same mistake the system keeps making, and it keeps making it in the same direction."

Bruce took the pages. "Who's preserving the originals?"

"I am. I learned that from an airline dispute about a lost violin. Never let anybody tell you the final record is final unless you've seen what they changed."

Tony raised an eyebrow. "Anika, remind me never to cross you."

"You already have. I'm a forgiving woman when it serves a purpose."

She walked away.

Bruce looked at the two signed sheets, one under the other. A process could be incorrect by accident. A record could be wrong because someone believed the process. And a person could decide that admitting the error would be worse than keeping it alive.

He put the pages back into the folder.

"We need to know which kind of wrong this is," he said.

Tony nodded. "Before somebody makes it permanent."
