# Chapter Twenty-Seven: Human Factors

By nine thirty, the terminal smelled of damp wool, overworked coffee machines, and fresh bread.

The bread had become a minor administrative controversy. A bakery near C12 had opened its emergency stores and distributed rolls without charge. Its manager had declined to sign the airport's reimbursement form until somebody explained whether the form would require a photograph of the recipients. The finance officer said that was standard practice. The manager said no one was getting photographed in exchange for breakfast. After twenty minutes of increasingly polite disagreement, Anika Bell took the form, crossed out the photography section, and wrote *PROVISIONS PROVIDED DURING GROUND STOP — CONFIRMED BY STAFF*.

"Will that be accepted?" the manager asked.

"It will be accepted by me," Bell replied. "If they want to argue, they can find a chair."

Tony, passing with a crate of cups, decided this was the most convincing demonstration of executive authority he'd seen in years.

He spent a moment watching the bakery workers hand out rolls. They were not smiling for a camera. They were tired, their white aprons stained with flour, their movements reduced to practical repetition. Take a roll. Ask whether the child wanted a smaller one. Offer water. Count what remained. It was the same procedure, again and again, and it worked because people were paying attention.

Asha approached with two envelopes under her arm.

"Could I get a second opinion?" she asked.

"I have many," Tony said. "They're often described as excessive."

"Not about the software. About what to call the problem."

She showed him a draft summary meant for the first staff debrief. At the top, under *incident causes*, someone had written **HUMAN ERROR: FAILURE TO VERIFY TRANSFER**.

Tony felt his expression change. "Who's the human?"

"Exactly. Imani didn't verify the handoff after the tablet told her it was complete. A marshal assumed the group had been received. My operator screen didn't show the people as present. Facilities had a closure order that the pilot inventory didn't carry. The test assessor found the problem and the fix was deferred."

"And the summary picked one person?"

"It picked an abstract phrase. No names. That makes it look less cruel, but maybe it's worse."

Tony read the paragraph again. He remembered how many times he'd seen the same explanation after a failure in one of his own companies. A worker had pressed the wrong button; an operator had missed an alert; a technician had not followed a procedure. Every one of those accounts could be true in part, and every one could be used to hide the larger question: what kind of system depended on flawless performance from people who had been given misleading instructions?

"Call it an unresolved causal sequence," he said. "Document the known failures separately. Then ask which controls were supposed to catch each one."

"That sounds very Bruce Wayne."

"That's the most hurtful thing anyone's said to me all morning."

"He was the one who told me not to use 'human error' without specifying what the person was asked to do and what information they had."

"Of course he was." Tony handed the paper back. "Fine. Keep his phrasing. It's better."

They took the draft to Leila, who corrected the heading without ceremony. She was gathering the separate statements from people who had been on duty during the transfer, then arranging for each to review their own words before signing. No one would be pressured to repeat a version of events supplied by a superior. The incident recorder had a box of fresh folders and an expression of determined misery.

"What are you going to do with the old draft?" Asha asked.

"Keep it," Leila said. "Mark it superseded. It matters that someone thought that was an adequate explanation at nine in the morning."

Tony looked impressed. "You have a ferocious relationship with documents."

"I used to work on the ramp," she answered. "You don't get to edit the weather report after a plane lands."

At the far table, Imani Cole sat with Elaine Wu, the journalist who had been stranded at Bellwether since the previous afternoon. Imani had agreed to speak after Leila assured her she could decline and that the airport would not make a statement on her behalf. The two women were not conducting an interrogation. Elaine's phone lay face down. A paper notebook rested between them.

"I saw the tablet say *complete*," Imani was explaining. "I'd practiced the move on the rehearsal system. In the practice run, I only got that message after a receiving person acknowledged the passengers. I thought the real system meant the same thing."

"Did you receive any instruction that the definitions had changed?" Elaine asked.

"I got a revised quick guide. It didn't explain that part."

"Do you think you should have stayed?"

Imani looked down at her hands. "Yes. If I'd known nobody had taken the handoff, I would have stayed. But I was told to move another group away from the windows. There were people there too."

"So you had two duties at once."

"And one screen told me the first duty was finished."

Elaine wrote the sentence down. Then she asked whether Imani wanted her name included. Imani hesitated a long time.

"Yes," she said at last. "But include what I said, not just that I left. If people are going to learn from this, they need to know what I saw."

Tony moved away. He did not want her to look up and find a famous man standing over a story that was hers to tell.

At C17, Nina Ortiz was showing the retired teacher her linked chain of paper passengers. There were new links now, one for every person she had met. Tony saw BRUCE and TONY near the beginning, followed by ASHA, LEILA, FRANKIE, ANIKA, CELIA, and a dozen more. She had drawn small symbols beside some names: a lamp for Anika, a cup for Leila, a little plane for Frankie.

"What did you draw for me?" Tony asked.

She showed him the paper strip. Beside TONY was a pencil.

"A pencil?"

"You kept writing things down."

"I have a chest full of machines and a global defense portfolio, and you remember me for a pencil."

Nina looked puzzled. "You didn't have any of that stuff here."

"Good point."

Bruce arrived carrying the last of the paper rosters that needed to be filed. He leaned down to inspect the chain.

"What did you draw for me?" he asked.

Nina held up his strip. It showed an ear.

"An ear," Bruce said.

"You listen."

For a moment something passed over his face that Tony could not name. Bruce looked at the drawing as though it were an unfamiliar kind of evidence.

"That's a fair assessment," he said finally.

Nina grinned and returned to her work.

Outside the windows, the first municipal inspection vehicles had reached the perimeter road. They moved slowly through rain. Airport operations announced that passenger road access remained restricted while drainage and debris checks continued. No flights had departed. Nobody who had waited all night could yet be promised a departure time.

Asha stepped beside the two men and looked at the chain across Nina's lap.

"I thought I wanted to make a system that didn't need people to keep checking it," she said.

"You still can make better systems," Tony told her. "Just don't make one that considers checking it a failure."

Bruce nodded. "And give the person who disagrees with the screen a way to be heard before something happens."

Asha watched Nina add another paper link.

"Maybe that's the specification," she said.

Tony smiled. "Put it in the contract."
# Chapter Twenty-Eight: Two Kinds of Armor

The first ordinary sound Tony heard after the storm was a spoon falling onto a saucer.

It happened in a café near Gate C19 at 10:16 in the morning. The terminal's lighting had been restored to its normal brightness, though several corridors remained closed. A worker behind the counter dropped the spoon, said a word Tony would have admired if he hadn't been drinking coffee, retrieved another, and went on making a latte. No alarm sounded. Nobody looked toward operations for permission to continue. A small human mistake had been corrected without a committee.

Tony sat across from Bruce at a table between the café and the windows. Outside, rain still washed over the runway, but the aircraft had reappeared out of the gray. The ground stop had not been lifted. Their original flights had been canceled, as had enough others to turn the departures board into a monument to disappointment. The airline had offered seats on a later service to New York, with no guarantee that it would operate until the weather cleared.

"Do you think we could convince somebody that this was a very elaborate networking event?" Tony asked.

"Only if they hadn't attended it."

"I knew there was a reason nobody invites you to product launches."

Bruce turned the paper cup between his hands. He had not slept since the previous morning. The exhaustion had made his expression less controlled, and the small lines around his eyes looked older than the rest of his face.

Tony noticed them because his own reflection in the café window was no better.

"What are you going to do when you get home?" he asked.

"Sleep."

"A dangerously ambitious answer."

"After that, find out whether our accessibility funding reached the people it was supposed to reach."

"I'm asking about you, Bruce. Not your foundation."

Bruce was quiet for a long time.

"I don't know," he said.

Tony looked at him, startled by the admission. "You really don't?"

"I have a schedule. That's not necessarily an answer."

"Oh. That's an interesting problem. I have a schedule too. Usually three schedules, depending on who's trying to keep me alive."

Bruce set his cup down. "You make jokes when something matters."

"You go silent when something matters. Between us, we make one reasonably functional person."

"Possibly."

Tony leaned back and glanced toward the far concourse. Nina was helping Celia fold their blankets. Anika Bell had returned to the C19 service podium, where the printer was operating again with all the confidence of a machine that had learned nothing from the night.

"It's strange," Tony said. "I used to think the worst thing that could happen to me was being unable to build something. I've spent so much of my life proving I can solve problems with my hands that I sometimes forget people aren't obliged to wait for me to invent an answer."

"You built things because you needed them," Bruce said.

"Some. Others because I wanted to prove I could."

"There's a difference."

"You sound familiar with it."

Bruce looked at him. The man's face was an elegant public composition, an expression useful at funerals, shareholder meetings, and charity dinners. In the quiet light of the café, it seemed almost painfully insufficient to contain whatever he was thinking.

"I learned early that money could solve many problems," Bruce said. "And some it couldn't even approach."

Tony thought of the cave where his life had narrowed to scraps of metal and the will of another man who refused to give up on him. He thought of how the lessons of that place had followed him into rooms filled with people who congratulated him for surviving rather than asking what survival had made him do.

"Yeah," he said. "I know that one."

They watched a baggage crew move across the apron under the supervision of an airfield vehicle. The workers wore reflective jackets that shone against the wet pavement. To Tony, who could name a hundred features of the aircraft they were servicing, the most interesting thing was the woman at the front of the crew who kept checking that everybody had room to move.

"I have a theory," he said.

"Of course you do."

"We both wear armor. Mine's just easier to photograph."

Bruce's hand went still on his cup.

Tony smiled without malice. "Relax. I'm not asking for a demonstration. I just think you've spent more time in dangerous places than your public relations department cares to discuss."

"Your public relations department has given up discussing dangerous places."

"Mine issues emergency statements with the breakfast menu."

Bruce looked toward the windows. "Armor can keep a person alive. It can also make it easier not to let anyone close enough to help."

"That wasn't in my engineering specification."

"It isn't always obvious until later."

Tony studied him. There were questions he could ask, hints he could press, little mysteries Bruce was unlikely to answer. For the first time in his adult life, Tony felt no need to turn another man's privacy into a problem he had the power to solve.

"Well," he said, "tonight we had to work without our favorite hardware."

"We did."

"And neither of us became completely useless."

"Speak for yourself. I was very useful carrying blankets."

"You were a menace to the blanket economy."

"Stay in touch," Tony said.

"I will."

"And tell your butler I want to meet him."

"He's already formed an opinion of you."

"That's unfair. We haven't met."

"He watched a news clip of you discussing the wet-floor sign."

Tony closed his eyes. "Please tell me there isn't footage."

"There is."

"Of course there is."

"He said you showed admirable restraint."

"That's a compliment."

"From him, a considerable one."

Tony smiled. "Tell him I accept the challenge."

Bruce moved toward the line, then stopped and looked back.

"Tony."

"Yeah?"

"Don't lose the pencil."

Tony pulled the short yellow airport pencil from his pocket and held it up. "Never. Most expensive lesson I've had all week, and it was free."

Bruce nodded once and joined the boarding queue.

Anika Bell checked his pass, thanked him for his patience, and wished him a safe flight. He responded with the same quiet courtesy he'd shown her at the beginning. The difference was that he now knew the scale of what she'd done between those two moments. She had held a hundred ordinary promises together through a night that kept trying to break them.

Tony watched Bruce disappear down the jet bridge. He felt an unusual absence at his side, the kind left by a conversation that had not finished. He looked toward the now-empty chair and wondered when he'd started looking forward to being contradicted.

His phone buzzed. A text from an unfamiliar number appeared.

*You still owe Nina two points. — B*

Tony stared at it and began to laugh.

He typed back: *Tell your butler I'm bringing my own chess set. And a better printer.*

A moment later: *Please don't.*

Tony slipped the phone into his pocket. Beyond the windows, an aircraft moved toward the runway. He stood, collected the last paper cup from beside his chair, and dropped it into the recycling bin.

Then he went to ask Anika Bell whether the remaining passengers at C19 needed anything before he boarded.

"They need to get on the aircraft," she said. "And so do you, Mr. Stark. You are now the last person on my list."

Tony looked at her clipboard. His name appeared on the manifest like everybody else's, checked against a seat, a flight, and a place in the boarding queue. No category of importance had been attached to it.

"The last passenger," he said. "That sounds more dramatic than it is."

"Most things do when you say them."

He took the boarding pass, thanked her, and walked down the jet bridge. Halfway to the aircraft he paused and looked back. Anika had already turned to a colleague, helping close the departure record. No audience. No cameras. Just the next job.

Tony continued toward the airplane. He had one final question for the attendant at the door: whether anybody needed help with a bag.

This time, he listened to the answer.

========== EPILOGUE ==========

Alfred left him with his breakfast. Bruce read the letter once more, then set the pencil in the drawer where he kept useful things that were too small to deserve a display case.

***

The second letter arrived at Stark's workshop that same afternoon.

Pepper brought it down herself, partly because she was curious and partly because Tony had ignored three calendar reminders while studying a prototype for a small battery-backed public notice board. The prototype was not his project. Asha had asked for comments on a list of design questions, and Tony had been forced to acknowledge that a good comment could be more valuable than a surprise redesign.

Pepper put the envelope on the workbench. "Personal mail. Handwritten. You have been receiving a suspicious amount of that lately."

"I assume it's fan correspondence."

"It's from a child."

"A particularly discerning fan."

She crossed her arms, which meant she intended to remain until the envelope had been opened.

Tony tore it carefully, trying not to damage the paper inside. There were two sheets. The first was a photocopy of a child's handmade book, its pages linked by colored tape. A drawing of two men at an airport gate occupied the center: one with dark hair and an exaggerated mustache-like smudge, the other with enormous ears. Between them stood a woman holding a clipboard. Behind them was a paper airplane.

The second sheet contained a letter in block capitals.

*DEAR MR. STARK AND MR. WAYNE,*

*I FINISHED MY AIRPORT BOOK. EVERYBODY GOT HOME EXCEPT THE PEOPLE WHO WERE STILL WAITING, AND THEN THEY GOT HOME LATER. THAT IS NOT THE SAME THING AS EVERYBODY AT ONCE.*

*MY AUNT SAID YOU HELPED. I THINK YOU HELPED TOO, BUT SO DID MS. BELL AND ASHA AND FRANKIE AND LEILA AND THE DOCTOR AND THE LADY WHO GAVE OUT BREAD. I PUT THEM IN THE BOOK FIRST BECAUSE THEY WERE WORKING EVEN WHEN NO ONE WAS LOOKING.*

*I MADE A NEW SURVEY. YOU ARE BOTH NINE OUT OF TEN NOW. MR. WAYNE GETS ANOTHER POINT FOR WRITING ME BACK. MR. STARK GETS ANOTHER POINT FOR THE CRACKERS AND THE ROBOT DRAWING, EVEN THOUGH IT STILL HAS TOO MANY FINGERS.*

*YOU DO NOT GET TEN BECAUSE YOU BOTH ARGUED TOO MUCH.*

*LOVE, NINA ORTIZ*

*P.S. THE PLANE FINALLY TOOK OFF. I DREW IT ON THE LAST PAGE.*

Tony read the last line twice and looked up. Pepper was smiling in a way that made the workshop seem warmer.

"Nine out of ten," he said. "I have launched satellites with worse evaluations."

"She seems to have understood you very clearly."

"She drew me with a mustache. I don't have a mustache."

"Perhaps she was being generous."

He held up the drawing. "And Bruce has ears the size of satellite dishes."

Pepper laughed. "Those are reasonably accurate."

Tony took his phone and photographed the letter, careful to keep the child's address out of the picture. He sent the image to Bruce with a single message: *Nine. We can do better.*

The reply arrived several minutes later.

*Ten requires arguing less.*

Tony typed back: *Then I refuse to compromise my principles.*

*That explains your score.*

He grinned at the screen. It was a good conversation. One that did not require either man to win.

Pepper picked up the preliminary design notes from the bench. "Are you going to build the display?"

"No. Asha's team is. I answered her questions about redundant status channels and insisted the manual notice be independently readable. I did not send an unsolicited shipping container of equipment."

"I'm proud of you."

"Please don't tell anyone. My brand will suffer."

She placed the notes down. "Are you going to finish lunch?"

"That's your transition? From institutional accountability to lunch?"

"That's how actual life works, Tony. People need to eat."

He looked at the child's letter again, at the paper airplane rising beyond the drawn windows. Nine weeks earlier, he would have been tempted to see the story as proof that all emergencies were solvable by enough cleverness. He knew better now. Cleverness had helped. So had patience, procedure, stubbornness, and the willingness of people with no public reputation to stand beside strangers in the dark.

"Yeah," he said. "They do."

He put the letter where he could see it, not framed, not filed as a trophy, simply propped beside his workbench. Then he went upstairs for lunch.

Far away, in an airport that smelled again of coffee and floor polish, the staff at Gate C19 were changing the paper in a printer. The new machine worked beautifully. It was quiet, fast, and perfectly ordinary.

Anika Bell watched it finish a page, checked that the words matched the passenger in front of her, and handed over a boarding pass.

"Safe travels," she said.

She waited until the traveler had actually taken it before she marked the transaction complete.

def inline_markup(t):
    t=html.escape(t,quote=False)
    t=re.sub(r'\*\*([^*]+)\*\*',r'<b>\1</b>',t)
    t=re.sub(r'\*([^*]+)\*',r'<i>\1</i>',t)
    t=t.replace('&#x27;',"'")
    return t

def paragraphs(md):
    for block in re.split(r'\n\s*\n',md.strip()):
        block=block.strip()
        if not block: continue
        if block.startswith('# '):
            yield ('heading',block[2:].strip())
        else:
            yield ('paragraph', ' '.join(x.strip() for x in block.splitlines()))


def on_first(c,doc):
    c.drawImage(str(root/'cover.jpg'),0,0,width=PAGE[0],height=PAGE[1])

def on_later(c,doc):
    c.setFont('Lato',8)
    c.setFillColor(colors.HexColor('#7C858A'))
    c.drawCentredString(PAGE[0]/2,0.42*inch,str(doc.page))

page_margins=dict(leftMargin=.66*inch,rightMargin=.66*inch,topMargin=.72*inch,bottomMargin=.68*inch)
doc=SimpleDocTemplate(str(file),pagesize=PAGE,title='Ground Stop',author='Unofficial original crossover fan fiction',subject='A Tony Stark & Bruce Wayne airport novel',**page_margins)
story=[]
# Full-bleed cover on first page, empty frame
story.append(Spacer(1,2))
story.append(PageBreak())
# title page
story += [Spacer(1,1.15*inch),Paragraph('GROUND STOP',styles['title']),Spacer(1,20),Paragraph('A Tony Stark &amp; Bruce Wayne Crossover Novel',styles['subtitle']),Spacer(1,1.1*inch),Paragraph('Two billionaires. One airport. No way out.',styles['center']),Spacer(1,1.6*inch),Paragraph('A complete standalone story<br/>Thirty chapters and an epilogue',styles['subtitle']),PageBreak()]
# Reader's note
story += [Spacer(1,.45*inch),Paragraph("Reader's Note",styles['notetitle']),Paragraph('<i>Ground Stop</i> is a standalone, unofficial crossover story featuring Tony Stark and Bruce Wayne. It is original fan fiction, not affiliated with, endorsed by, or authorized by the rights holders of those characters. Bellwether International and the supporting cast are original to this story.',styles['bodyfirst']),Spacer(1,.5*inch),Paragraph('<i>For the people who stay.</i>',styles['center']),PageBreak()]
# TOC links in chapter flow; bookmarks via anchor inside heading paragraph
part_heads={1:('Part One: The Waiting','All flights hold.'),9:('Part Two: The Missing','No one should have to prove they are still here.'),21:('Part Three: The Count','The numbers do not carry themselves home.')}
chapters=sorted((root/'chapters').glob('*.md'))
assert len(chapters)==30
story += [Paragraph('CONTENTS',styles['notetitle']),Spacer(1,10)]
for i,p in enumerate(chapters,1):
 if i in part_heads:
  h,_=part_heads[i]
  story.append(Paragraph(inline_markup(h),styles['tocpart']))
 t=p.read_text().splitlines()[0].removeprefix('# ').strip()
 story.append(Paragraph(f'<link href="#ch{i}">{html.escape(t)}</link>',styles['toc']))
story.append(Paragraph('<link href="#epilogue">Epilogue: Two Letters, Nine Weeks Later</link>',styles['toc']))
story.append(PageBreak())
for i,p in enumerate(chapters,1):
 if i in part_heads:
  h,sub=part_heads[i]
  story += [Spacer(1,1.6*inch),Paragraph(inline_markup(h),styles['part']),Spacer(1,32),Paragraph(inline_markup(sub),styles['partsub']),PageBreak()]
 else:
  story.append(PageBreak())
 paras=list(paragraphs(p.read_text(encoding='utf-8')))
 assert paras[0][0]=='heading'
 story.append(Paragraph(f'<a name="ch{i}"/>'+inline_markup(paras[0][1]),styles['h1']))
 for j,(typ,text) in enumerate(paras[1:]):
  sty=styles['bodyfirst'] if j==0 else styles['body']
  story.append(Paragraph(inline_markup(text),sty))
# epilogue
story.append(PageBreak())
paras=list(paragraphs((root/'epilogue.md').read_text(encoding='utf-8')))
story.append(Paragraph('<a name="epilogue"/>'+inline_markup(paras[0][1]),styles['h1']))
for j,(_,text) in enumerate(paras[1:]):
 if text=='***':
  story.append(Spacer(1,14))
 else:
  story.append(Paragraph(inline_markup(text),styles['bodyfirst'] if j==0 else styles['body']))
doc.build(story,onFirstPage=on_first,onLaterPages=on_later)
print(file,file.stat().st_size,'bytes', 'chapters',len(chapters))

# GROUND STOP
*A Tony Stark & Bruce Wayne Crossover Novel*

<text color="secondary" size="sm">Complete story · Approximately 42,000 words · 30 chapters + epilogue</text>

<box maxWidth="300px">
  <AsyncImage query="dark atmospheric empty airport terminal nighttime storm runway amber gate departure board cinematic book cover" aspectRatio="2:3"/>
</box>

**Two billionaires. One airport. No way out.**

A storm grounds Tony Stark and Bruce Wayne at the same airport. What begins as a battle of sarcasm, engineering, and detective instincts turns into a mystery involving passengers an airport system has somehow stopped counting.

The novel includes their escalating rivalry, an ensemble of stranded passengers and airport workers, a complete central mystery, and a proper ending.

<box gap={2}>
  <row align="center" justify="between">
    <text weight="medium">EPUB — Apple Books, Kindle-compatible readers</text>

# Chapter Nine: The Founder Problem

The first thing Tony Stark learned about a terminal running on reduced power was that everything became louder.

He had always assumed the opposite. Less electricity meant fewer machines, fewer machines meant fewer noises. It was a beautifully incorrect theory. In the near-dark of Bellwether International, with the main ventilation fans cycling down and the soft mechanical music of travel suddenly absent, individual sounds rose out of the crowd with an almost indecent clarity. Someone coughed. A wheel on a luggage cart clicked every third revolution. Rain struck a metal service hatch six hundred feet away, and the echoes carried all the way to the food court.

The frightened voices were the worst. Not the shouts. People raised their voices when they believed somebody might answer. It was the conversations held too softly, a mother assuring her son that the building was safe while looking up at its glass roof, a man telling his sister over the phone that there was no need to worry, nothing serious, although the lights were going out around him.

Tony reached the aid station to find Dr. Nia Okafor checking the respiratory-assistance equipment while a volunteer held a flashlight over a clipboard.

"What's happened?" she asked before he had spoken.

"Local power transfer. Environmental reporting for the north gallery dropped. Building engineers are checking the actual equipment. I don't have a confirmed physical failure yet."

She looked at him for half a second. "That's an answer I can use."

"I rehearsed it on the way over. Left out three theories and a joke about the Federal Aviation Administration."

"Keep practicing."

"You say that like you think there's hope for me."

She had already turned to the volunteer. "Tell operations our portable oxygen supplies are accounted for. Tell them I can send a clinician with the first authorized team, but I won't send anyone into a room with unknown air conditions without a safety briefing."

"I'll carry the message," Tony said.

"There are radios."

"I'm aware. I have a complicated relationship with being useful."

She studied him. Not as a patient or celebrity. As a man who seemed to have misplaced the rules of ordinary human interaction.

"Then here's your task," she said. "Find out whether the person on the other end of that intercom was standing with the patient he described, or reporting from somewhere else. If we're planning support for a woman with breathing trouble, location matters."

Tony nodded. A real question. A narrow one. He could work with that.

He started toward operations, then came back and placed a sealed bottle of water on her desk.

"For you," he said. "Not a medical intervention. Merely hydration."

"That's a first."

"My lawyers will be relieved."

By the time he reached the conference suite, the backup lighting had given the room the color of an old photograph. Leila Moreno had three radios talking at once and a phone wedged between shoulder and ear. The assistant who had brought in bottled water earlier was now assigning numbers to stacks of paper. Asha Desai was staring at a graph with the expression of somebody who knew exactly what a machine ought to do and was discovering that the machine disagreed.

Bruce Wayne stood beside the printed August floor plan. He had placed a strip of white tape across the center, separating the parts that were definitely accessible from those that might not be. The tape wasn't straight. Somehow that made Tony trust it more.

"The young doctor needs to know which half of the gallery Frankie was calling from," Tony said. "And whether the woman with breathing trouble was with him."

"I'm getting a new report from facilities," Moreno answered. "If I have it, so will she."

Cass Finch was at the far table. She had opened a second laptop and was trying to persuade a phone call to remain connected. Tony heard fragments: *contract exposure*, *incident-class normalization*, *time to recovery*. When she saw him, she closed the call without saying goodbye.

"I have a question for you," he said.

"I thought that was why you came."

"There was a design review. Somebody sat in a room and said, 'This status means complete, but the person is still physically there.' How many people were at that meeting?"

"You would need to review the change log."

"I would love to."

"That isn't a request your legal status entitles you to make."

"No. Leila's is. Mine is just a very expensive suggestion."

Moreno looked up, distracted but not absent. "Cass, preservation of incident records is part of your response obligation. If anybody has proposed deleting, refreshing, or replacing the current state, I need to know before it happens."

"Our failover procedures can roll the service back to a validated baseline," Finch said. "That doesn't necessarily destroy records."

"Does it risk changing the view of the incident?"

"It can alter temporary displays."

"Then we don't do it until you've preserved what we have and my team understands the consequence."

Finch nodded. "I'll advise engineering."

Tony wanted to ask what *advise* meant in a world in which a senior executive could issue instructions. He chose instead to watch her hands. They weren't shaking. They were too still, the fingers squared against the edge of the laptop, as if her body had decided to contain movement by force.

Bruce saw the same thing. Tony could tell because Bruce looked away first.

"Can we have a word?" Bruce asked Tony.

"I just got here. My fan club will be devastated."

"Outside."

They stepped into the hall. A line of travelers filed past carrying blankets toward the center concourse. A man in a business suit had a child asleep against his shoulder; the child couldn't have been his, Tony thought, judging by the stranger's awkward, careful grip. The man nodded at them without recognition, intent on keeping the sleeping head from striking the wall.

"What?" Tony asked.

"You're pushing Finch because you're angry."

"She's helping hide forty-three people on a screen. I have earned the right to be angry."

"You don't know yet whether she designed the exception, signed it, concealed it, or found out about it when we did."

"You're going to make me recite that every ten minutes, aren't you?"

"Only until you stop treating a pattern as a verdict."

Tony laughed without humor. "You want to know what the founder problem is, Wayne? Everybody makes room for you to be certain. They stop arguing because certainty looks like competence when it's wearing a tailored suit. Then the ones who are wrong enough, loud enough, and rich enough end up with their ideas installed in buildings."

"I know," Bruce said.

"Do you?"

Bruce looked through the glass wall into the operations room. "Every time somebody tells me a Wayne-funded system is working, I ask who was permitted to say it wasn't."

Tony had expected a challenge. He disliked being handed an answer he recognized.

"And what happens?"

"Usually I discover we didn't ask enough people."

For a moment they stood together while an automatic door tried and failed to close. A porter crossed to it, moved a suitcase out of the track, and the door shut obediently. Nobody applauded. Nobody called it innovation.

"I know I can be a problem," Tony said finally.

"That's a start."

"I also know people get killed when everyone waits for the right committee."

"That's why Leila is doing something. She's getting a lawful, physically safe route opened. There's a difference between delay and care."

"You always this patient?"

"No."

Tony looked at him. There was enough plain truth in that single syllable to cancel the next three jokes Tony had prepared.

Back inside, Jonah Price had arrived with a maintenance technician and a soaked clipboard. He smelled of rain and metal. His gray sleeves were dark to the elbows.

"Two separate findings," he told Moreno. "The public bridge shutter is still electrically isolated pending an inspection. We found water in the lower service passage, but it isn't entering from the shoreline. Roof runoff is backing up because the old drain line isn't moving it. A repair crew is working at the collection point. As for the gallery air, the status link failed when the circuit transferred. That doesn't prove the fans stopped. The local indicators are behind the shutter."

"Can we restore communication with the room?"

"Maybe through the old wired station if we can get a temporary line from the service side. I don't want anybody opening an electrical cabinet in standing water. I'll take my people through the dry approach."

"Give me the route and the hazards before you go," Moreno said.

"Already marked." He unfolded the clipboard.

Tony watched Bruce turn the page toward Jonah rather than point at a different solution. Bruce had an answer for nearly everything, Tony suspected, but his actual skill might be knowing which answers belonged to other people.

"Jonah," Tony said, "if the public bridge checks out, is there a way to move medical supplies across without using the service passage?"

"A hand trolley over the bridge, provided the floor's sound and a fire marshal clears the route. Nothing fancy."

"I have never been so happy to hear the words 'hand trolley.'"

Jonah gave him an amused, unimpressed look. "You should spend more time in maintenance."

"I'd bankrupt you in a week."

"No," Jonah said. "We'd take away your purchase authority."

Asha laughed. It was a sharp, relieved sound that startled all three men.

Then the radio on Moreno's desk clicked.

"Operations, this is north service. Somebody just tried to push a safety all-clear to the gallery from the contractor console. It didn't go through because the zone's held under manual incident control. Want us to release the hold?"

"No," Moreno said. "Keep it. Who requested the clearance?"

There was a pause long enough for Tony to see Finch turn toward her laptop.

"Identified as the continuity support desk," came the answer. "No individual's name on the notice."

Finch stood. "That's a recovery workflow, not a physical clearance."

Moreno looked at her. "Then whoever wrote it needs to stop using language that the people operating this airport understand to mean the building is safe."

Finch said nothing.

Tony could feel the familiar anger coming back. He could also feel Bruce's attention, not restraining him but waiting to see what he would do with it.

He asked Moreno, "What can I do that doesn't make this worse?"

She looked genuinely surprised.

"Go to the waiting area," she said. "Find Anika. Ask her to organize enough volunteers for a full manual census of Terminal C. One count per zone, duplicate checked, by people, not just tickets. And don't make yourself the person in charge."

"So: carry paper, listen, don't announce the invention of counting."

"Precisely."

Tony saluted with two fingers and left.

Behind him, Bruce said, very quietly, "You're learning."

Tony did not turn around.

"I'm going to pretend that sounded less like a dog-training manual," he called back.

But he was smiling as he walked away, and the smile was not one he needed a camera to see.
EOF

# Chapter Ten: Lost and Found

Bruce found the first missing person at the counter where passengers reported missing property.

The desk had been closed for the evening, but its steel roller screen was only halfway down. Behind it, an airport employee was eating crackers from a vending-machine package and watching an old television mounted on the wall. The picture showed the storm passing over the shoreline, an unbroken field of weather with a red warning band across the bottom. The sound was muted. A folding chair supported a cardboard box marked FOUND TODAY.

Bruce had come because the woman at Gate C18 had been trying to find her husband for twenty minutes and nobody had known which telephone extension to use. At the lost-property desk he discovered two things. The airport had a telephone directory printed in a binder. And the employee had seen the husband.

"Gray beard? Navy cap? Carrying a case with a broken yellow strap?" the employee asked.

"Yes."

"He went to the interior seating by C12. Helped a woman get a stroller over the ramp. I told him the baggage office was closed, and he said he didn't need baggage, he needed somewhere to sit."

Bruce wrote the location on a small card. "Thank you."

"You Bruce Wayne?"

"Yes."

"I thought so. You look more tired than on television."

"Television is generous."

"This place isn't." She tipped the box toward him. "Want to see what's left of the evening?"

He saw one child's mitten, three phones, a wallet containing a photograph of two women outside a beach house, two pairs of spectacles, and an astonishing quantity of single earbuds.

"It all belongs to somebody," she said. "The phones are easy if they ring. The little things are harder. People forget they lost them until they're halfway home."

"Do you keep a log?"

"Of course. If you lose the record, you haven't found the thing. You've just moved the problem."

Bruce looked down at the binder in her hands. Each item had a time, location, description, initials of the employee who accepted it, and a shelf number. It was not sophisticated. It was complete.

"May I take a picture of the blank form?"

"You can ask my supervisor. Why?"

"I think the incident team would benefit from something like it."

She pointed a cracker at him. "Tell them to use a pencil for the location until somebody verifies it. People swear they lost things in places they never went."

Bruce smiled despite himself. "I will."

When he returned to C18 with the husband's location, the woman squeezed his wrist in gratitude and hurried away. Her seat had been occupied by an untidy pile of blankets. Beside it, Nina Ortiz sat making a chain from paper boarding passes, each piece folded through the next. Her aunt Celia was speaking with Anika Bell.

"How many people have you counted?" Bruce asked.

Bell looked at a sheet on her clipboard. "One hundred and thirty-two here and at C17 combined, including eight staff temporarily assigned to the zone. Separate columns. Some came from C19, some from other gates. I've got a name or physical description for every person who'll give me one, and a mark for everybody who won't."

"And you know which counts overlap?"

"Yes. Unlike the computer, I am capable of crossing out a mistake."

"You might want a revision record," Bruce said.

She gave him a look.

"Please don't tell me how to make a list."

"I was going to ask you to help other teams do it."

"Oh." She considered. "That, I can do."

Nina's chain extended from one knee to the floor. She held up two linked pieces. "I figured out why the number went down."

Bruce crouched beside her. "Tell me."

"I made forty-three people out of these. They're all on a chain. If I put the chain in the box, nobody's gone, right?"

"Right."

She tucked the chain into her backpack and zipped it. "But if I count only what's on the floor, the number goes down. It's not the same as people disappearing."

"No. It isn't."

"Then why do grown-ups keep saying forty-three people disappeared?"

Bruce considered the question carefully. "Because that's what a computer made it look like. Some of the grown-ups are trying to find the difference."

"You should tell them it's a bad way to say it."

"I will."

She looked pleased. "Good."

The comparison unsettled Bruce more than it ought to have. He had spent years trying to restore names to stories that other people had reduced to crime statistics. An unsolved murder was not a cold case to the people who set another place at the table without meaning to. A person missing from a shelter was not a minus sign. He knew that better than almost anyone. Yet he had repeated the shorthand without noticing what it did.

Nina pulled a blue pen from her backpack. "Can you write your name on one? I'm collecting people I met during the storm."

He took the pen and wrote BRUCE on a strip. She made him wait while she searched for another one.

"The other man wrote TONY and drew a little robot," she said. "He said it was anatomically accurate. But robots don't have mustaches."

"Some do."

"That doesn't make it a good drawing."

"No," Bruce said. "It doesn't."

She slid his strip through Tony's and made another link. Bruce examined it in silence while Celia watched him with a shy, tired smile.

"She's been making lists since she was four," Celia said. "Things in the refrigerator, things in the sky, all the ways her uncle says the same swear word."

"That's an ambitious list," Bruce said.

"Especially in traffic."

Celia's smile vanished as a faint alarm sounded from the north end of the terminal. It was a different chime, lower and longer than C0. A staff member pressed a headset to her ear and began walking quickly.

"Is that the people in the old room?" Celia asked.

"I don't know," Bruce said, and found that the true answer steadied her more than a false reassurance would have.

He stood. "Stay here with Anika. She'll tell you if the instructions change."

At operations, the alarms had been categorized: no evacuation signal, no active fire event, a failed environmental status check in an inaccessible annex. Jonah was sending a second maintenance crew to establish independent readings from the dry bridge approach. Moreno had moved from the conference table to a wall chart on which the entire terminal was being counted in chalk. There were three kinds of mark: confirmed, provisional, and unassigned. Nothing could become confirmed simply because a digital number agreed with it.

"Why chalk?" Bruce asked.

"Pens kept running out," Moreno said. "Also, we can all see it."

Asha stood beside an enormous paper envelope. "Imani's transfer roster," she said. "The forty-three. All the marks she made when people entered the gallery. But one of the signatures is not a name."

Bruce inspected it. In the final column, beside line thirty-nine, was a diagonal mark shaped like a backwards seven. The associated traveler had been described as a wheelchair user, female, gray hair, green scarf. Her companion had supplied a surname, written as *Varela* or *Valera*. The handwriting was poor and the ink had bled.

"Did anyone ask her name?" Bruce asked.

"Imani says the transfer happened quickly. The woman was exhausted."

"We can't determine identity from this."

"I know. I've compared the airline mobility assistance manifest. There are three possibilities and a fourth passenger not on the list."

"Show the companion's details."

The adjacent column contained *A. Varela — assistant*, and a phone number missing its final digit. Bruce looked at the entry. A person could be on a roster and still be lost to the people who wanted to find them.

He was about to ask Asha to contact the relevant airline help desks when Finch entered. She had removed the clear poncho. Her navy jacket was damp across the shoulders, and one of her red glasses' arms was held in place with a strip of tape.

"Leila, our corporate continuity team wants an incident statement by midnight," she said.

"They can wait for a statement that tells the truth."

"There are obligations under the contract."

"There are forty-three passengers and one employee in a room I cannot safely reach yet," Moreno replied. "The contract doesn't take priority over them."

"It isn't that simple."

"Nothing tonight is simple."

Bruce intervened before the two women could become locked in an argument that had no useful exit.

"Ms. Finch. The transfer roster includes a woman who could not give her full name. Do you have an original intake record from the relocation staff?"

Finch blinked. "The tablet entry would be attached to the incident."

"May we have it? Through operations."

"The tablet data is included in the rollback package."

"Then preserve that package as it is now."

"That's already being addressed."

"Who's doing it?"

For the first time, she looked genuinely tired. "Mr. Wayne, do you understand how many people have called me in the last hour demanding that I fix the colored rectangles on a screen? Our contract has penalty clauses. The airport commissioner wants a restoration estimate. Our corporate office wants assurance that our systems did not cause the environmental fault. I am trying to keep an entire incident from becoming a commercial collapse while the floor moves under my feet."

Bruce allowed her to finish. "I understand why you're frightened," he said.

Her expression hardened. "I'm not frightened."

"Then tell us the name of the person preserving the records."

She looked at him, furious now, but the fury lasted only a moment. "Evan Lowe. He's started a manual export of the incident ledger. The export requires permission from the airport account holder. Asha has the operator certificate, but not the data custodian's."

"Who has that?" Moreno asked.

"Deputy Commissioner Kestrel."

Moreno checked the clock. 23:56.

"Fine," she said. "Get him here. Meanwhile we use the paper."

The little room grew still. Outside it, a thousand people were huddled on airport chairs, on floors, under blankets, in the endless fluorescent twilight of an unfinished journey.

Bruce returned the roster to its envelope and wrote on the front: **FORTY-THREE REPORTED. NAMES UNVERIFIED. EMPLOYEE ADDITIONAL.**

He thought of the woman at lost property, her ledger, and the certainty that one day each orphaned glove would belong to a particular hand again.

Asha watched him write. "That sounds terrible."

"It's accurate," Bruce said. "We can make it better."
EOF

# Chapter Eleven: Thirty-Seven Minutes of Dark

At six minutes after midnight, the terminal lost the argument it had been having with the wind.

The overhead lamps did not explode or vanish with cinematic obedience. They flickered once, twice, held a watery yellow for a count of five, and then went out in sections. Emergency strips along the floor came up, bathing the passageways in a light that made every face seem composed of sharp edges and deep recesses. The bright advertising screens died. The shops became dark arrangements of glass. The long arch of the terminal roof disappeared into a ceiling of reflected storm clouds.

Then, for one extraordinary second, nobody spoke.

Tony had heard silence after explosions, when the ears could not yet agree that the world was still making sounds. This was different. The people at Bellwether International had made a collective discovery that they were inside something much larger than themselves, and each was waiting for another person to decide how frightened they ought to be.

A little girl near the charging station said, "Oh."

Someone else began to cry.

The public address system clicked back to life on backup power. "Ladies and gentlemen. This is Bellwether Airport Operations. A portion of Terminal C is operating on emergency lighting. Please remain in your assigned shelter area. Airport staff are checking building systems. Do not move into darkened corridors without assistance. We will provide updates."

Leila Moreno's voice. Clear, unsentimental, with enough human breath in it that the announcement sounded like a person speaking to people rather than a machine speaking to inventory.

"That's a hell of an announcement," Tony said quietly.

"She wrote it herself," Anika Bell replied. She had appeared at his elbow with a lantern, three clipboards, and the face of a woman who would gladly fight the weather if it could be made to stand in line.

"We're restarting the count," she said. "My last one was at eleven forty-seven. Everybody stays where they are until I can check who moved."

"I can help."

"You can carry a lantern."

"I've been promoted from wet-floor sign."

"Don't get ambitious."

They moved down the row, calling for people to respond and checking those who couldn't. Some travelers held up boarding passes; Bell gently told them she needed to see human beings, not paper. The tired young father who had borrowed a phone to call home pointed out that his two children were asleep under the same blanket. An elderly woman tapped Tony's sleeve and asked whether the dark would last a long time.

"I don't know," Tony said. "The emergency lights are doing their job. Someone's checking the rest."

She nodded, satisfied enough to lean back again.

"You could've told her ten minutes," Bell murmured.

"You would've fired me."

"Probably."

At C17, Nina Ortiz was sitting in Celia's lap with her blue backpack clutched to her chest. The little paper chain had emerged from one side of its zipper.

"Mr. Mustache Robot," Nina called softly.

Tony bent toward her. "It's a precision illustration."

"Your robot has six fingers."

"Redundancy."

She was about to argue when a distant mechanical clang sounded from the north wall. It was answered by a low shudder that ran through the floor. Celia held Nina closer. Tony straightened.

"Facilities," Bell said before he could ask. "They're isolating a service zone. Don't speculate at the children."

"I wasn't going to."

"Your face was."

Tony had always thought his face was under excellent control. He was beginning to suspect that it had a more honest relationship with the world than he did.

The next emergency announcement provided no new estimate. Tony finished the count and delivered it to Bell, who had set up a checkpoint under the dead departure screen. One hundred and thirty-one confirmed in her zone now, one traveler escorted to the aid station, eight staff noted separately. She signed the sheet with time and location. She passed him a copy to carry to operations.

"Don't fold it," she said.

He looked at the paper. "Does it have royal blood?"

"It has ink that smudges."

"So, nobility of a different kind."

He carried it flat between both hands.

The corridor outside operations was crowded with people doing their jobs in low light. A police officer held a flashlight so an electrician could read a diagram. Two custodians laid absorbent pads across the floor without being asked. A cook came down the hall carrying a tray of hot rolls and distributing them to anyone in uniform. Tony took one at the cook's insistence and burnt his mouth because his most advanced technological achievements had never been enough to prevent him from eating food too quickly.

Inside the suite, a portable lamp cast shadows over the paper maps. Jonah had drawn a thick red line between the public bridge and the low service passage.

"The bridge is dry," he reported. "The shutter isn't. The motor housing got damp from the overflowing drainage collection. We have disconnected that circuit. Once we complete the structural and fire clearance, we can use the approved alternate manual opening method with the trained team. Nobody here is going to start pulling things apart in the dark."

"How long?" Moreno asked.

"I'm not promising a clock. The people in the gallery deserve a door that opens into a safe route, not a new problem."

Tony set the roll call down. The paper looked absurdly ordinary among the technical reports. He had brought back names from a section of the terminal without creating anything, inventing anything, or improving anything. He had never been so pleased to deliver a piece of paper.

Asha sat at a separate table. She had a wired handset connected to a temporary communication point approved by the facilities team. Every few seconds she lifted it and listened, then wrote a time on her pad.

"Anything?" Tony asked.

"The station has power at this end. At the other end, unknown. We can hear a faint click every couple of minutes, but not a voice. I'm not certain whether that's somebody lifting the receiver or interference from the circuit."

"Could you put a simple call-tone through the line?"

"Already did. Facilities says it's permitted at this voltage, but somebody on the far side has to be near the handset to hear it."

Tony rubbed his eyes. A memory rose uninvited: the cramped room in a cave, metal laid out on a workbench, a battery capable of more than the parts had a right to promise. He hated that memory because of what it had taught him. He had learned he could build astonishing things under pressure. The world had since encouraged him to believe that building something was the correct answer to every pressure.

Here he had no armor and no private network, only two safe communication points, a room full of people, and an apprentice who understood the airport better than he did.

"What do you need?" he asked Asha.

"Somebody to stand here while I rest my hand. I've been holding this receiver for half an hour. If it rings, press that button, call me. Don't change any settings."

"I can do that."

"Good. I'm going to get water."

She left the handset in his hand and walked away. Tony stood in the dim light and listened to the almost inaudible sound of distance. The emergency lighting made the room feel like an underwater station. The storm kept moving above them, and somewhere on the other side of a hundred old decisions forty-three travelers were waiting for something to open.

At 00:29, the line clicked.

Tony went still.

"Gallery station? This is airport operations. Can you hear me?"

Static.

He pressed the call indicator. "If anyone's there, this is Tony. No, not the Tony you're expecting, unless you were expecting one. We're working on getting to you."

A scratch of sound. Then a man's voice, distant and breathless.

"Stark?"

"Yes. Wasn't planning on the fan-club tour. Who's this?"

"Frankie. Frankie Saye. The first man said he was getting help."

"He did. We're here. Do you know how many people are with you?"

"On my side? Twenty-seven. I got the rest over the divider before the lights changed. Some of them are with a woman called Elena. Or Alena. I couldn't hear. There are children in there too, I think."

Tony looked up sharply. Bruce, who had just entered, reached for a pencil and began writing on the margin of the map.

"Do you have the woman who needs help breathing?" Tony asked.

"No. She's across the divider. I hear them calling every few minutes, but the door between us has a latch on the far side. They're taking care of her, far as I can tell."

"Can you ask them to answer by making noise?"

Bruce shook his head, then pointed to the earpiece. The line was weak. Don't prolong it unnecessarily.

Tony changed course. "Frankie, are you wet? Is water getting into the room you're in?"

"Not here. The other side had water near the windows. We put towels down. The air's getting close. Some folks are frightened."

"That's understandable. We're sending people as soon as the route is cleared. Please conserve your strength and stay near the receiver if you can do it safely."

"Tell them not to forget us."

Tony closed his eyes. He could have promised a rescue time. He could have said they had it under control. He could have recited every comforting lie he had ever heard, and nobody in the room would have blamed him for it.

"We know you're there," he said instead. "We've got your name. And we're not closing the count until we see you."

There was a long, uneven breath on the line.

"All right," Frankie said.

Then the connection failed again.

At 00:43 the main lights returned to half power. The entire interval had lasted thirty-seven minutes. Nobody cheered. The numbers were not good enough for that. The maintenance status showed partial recovery, the gallery was still inaccessible, and the people inside still needed help.

Tony lowered the receiver.

Bruce had written *27 on Frankie's side / 16 on other side? / count unconfirmed / air close / water at windows / respiratory patient beyond divider*. Beneath it he had underlined **44 INCLUDING FRANKIE — TO VERIFY**.

"Good call," Bruce said.

Tony stared at the note. "I didn't fix anything."

"You listened."

"Doesn't sound like enough."

"Not yet," Bruce said. "But it was exactly what he needed us to do first."
EOF

# Chapter Twelve: The Break Between Men

Bruce Wayne had once been told that stubbornness was a luxury available only to people who could afford the consequences.

The man who told him had been Alfred, which made the remark both memorable and impossible to dismiss. Alfred had been attempting to convince a fourteen-year-old Bruce to attend a school concert instead of spending the evening reviewing every police report he could find on an unsolved robbery. The lesson had been gentle. Bruce had resented it for years.

At one in the morning, in an airport full of sleeping strangers, he found himself thinking of it again.

Tony was at the end of the corridor outside the operations suite, speaking into his phone. He had secured a brief connection to Pepper, and his face reflected the blue light of the screen.

"No, not an evacuation aircraft," he was saying. "Ground stop is real, nobody's flying through this. I want a team in standby after the roads reopen. Portable environmental monitoring, communication units, medical kits, whatever the city requests. We make a list. We don't arrive with a parade."

He listened.

"I'm aware that this sounds like me asking permission. Try to enjoy it while it lasts."

Bruce turned away to give him privacy. It seemed a small thing to grant someone who could afford private islands.

He entered the conference suite, where Leila Moreno was talking with Dorian Kestrel, the airport's deputy commissioner. Kestrel was a lean man with a silver tie and tired eyes. He had the alert posture of someone whose work often took place in rooms where everything was discussed before anything was decided.

"I appreciate the urgency," Kestrel said, "but if the bridge is not certified for occupancy and somebody is injured while we force it, our liability becomes catastrophic."

"It is already an emergency access route in the signed August plan," Moreno replied. "Facilities is evaluating the actual condition. Nobody is proposing an unauthorized entry. I need you to stop describing the work as forcing a bridge."

"The newer routing overlay supersedes the old plan."

"Show me the approved revision."

"I'm requesting it."

"So am I. Until we have it, the signed safety plan and the building team's findings are what we can act on."

Kestrel's gaze went to the paper envelopes covering the table. "Why are we operating without the central dashboard?"

"Because it says the place we're trying to rescue people from is empty."

"That needs correction."

"It does," Moreno said. "Correcting the number doesn't open the door."

He sighed as if she had made a fair but inconvenient point. "What do you need from me?"

"Authority to preserve the full vendor incident record, including the original status changes. The data custodian must not let a routine rollback alter the record. And an incident-log entry that the operational count is manual until reconciled."

"The legal team will ask whether that implies the automated service failed."

"It did fail to show those people. Record that fact. The cause is a separate question."

Bruce stood near the wall until Kestrel noticed him. "Mr. Wayne. I wasn't told you'd joined incident command."

"I haven't," Bruce said. "Ms. Moreno is responsible for it."

"Good. You understand why we have to be careful."

"I understand why careful decisions need to be made by the people with authority and evidence."

It was not the endorsement Kestrel had requested. His eyes narrowed, but he turned back to the paperwork.

Bruce watched the deputy commissioner initial the preservation hold. It was the right decision, and it cost him perhaps six seconds. Bruce thought about how many conversations had been held to avoid those six seconds.

When Tony returned, he went directly to the wall map.

"Pepper has a logistics team ready when access is possible. I've told her no deployment without airport or municipal request."

"That's generous," Kestrel said.

"It's a contingency," Tony replied. "Not a press release."

"We may be able to discuss a partnership."

"We can discuss anything after somebody has counted the people in that annex."

Bruce saw it happen: Kestrel's face closed against the tone, and Tony's face hardened in response. Two men used to directing resources had recognized authority in one another and mistaken it for competition.

"We have the preservation hold," Bruce said, to break the exchange.

Tony stared at the initialed paper. "Great. Can we get to the room?"

"Jonah is still evaluating the access," Moreno said. "The bridge appears sound, but part of the shutter mechanism remains isolated. Fire safety wants confirmation that opening it won't compromise the zone."

"How long?"

"Until it is safe."

"There are people inside whose air is getting bad."

"I know."

"We know the corridor's dry. We know the bridge can carry weight. We have a maintenance crew and local controls. Why can't we get them out?"

"Because we don't yet know whether lifting that shutter would make their situation worse," Bruce answered.

Tony turned sharply. "I asked Leila."

"And she's answered you three times."

"Then perhaps the fourth time somebody can explain how waiting is safer than doing something."

Moreno looked at him with a flat, exhausted expression. "Because if we compromise a fire compartment during a weather emergency while the environmental system is unstable, we may create another hazard in the route we need to use. Jonah's people are not slow because they don't care. They are slow because they have to be right about where they send everyone."

Tony stopped. "Fine."

The word was not agreement. It was a door closed too hard.

Bruce followed him into the hall a minute later, finding him standing beneath a dead departures screen. The screen reflected the line of emergency lights along the floor, like a river that had forgotten how to flow.

"You didn't help," Tony said without looking at him.

"I wasn't trying to make you feel better."

"I'm aware. It's one of your more developed talents."

"You're treating people who know the building as though they need you to discover urgency for them."

"And you're treating procedure like a moral virtue."

"Safety isn't an aesthetic preference."

"Neither is getting people out before they run out of air."

Bruce took a breath. "I am not indifferent to them."

"I didn't say you were. I said you're willing to sit in a hallway and wait for a clean answer while somebody else runs out of options."

The accusation landed harder than it should have. Bruce thought of parents waiting outside an alley that would never be made safe, of children who would lose someone because another person had insisted on one more ordinary hour. The memories did not help him reason. They made him angry.

"And you think the answer is always to put yourself between the danger and the person?" he asked.

"Sometimes that's the only answer."

"And sometimes your need to be the answer turns everybody else into a supporting character in your story."

Tony's expression changed. The humor vanished as if someone had switched it off from a remote control.

"You don't know me," he said.

"I know what you've shown me."

"Then maybe look harder."

They stood silent. Two men who had spent fortunes building ways to prevent other people from suffering, both offended to discover that somebody might see the fear beneath the machinery.

Bruce could have apologized. He should have. Instead he looked toward the wall and said, "This isn't about you."

"No," Tony replied. "It isn't. Remember that when you make another speech about how much I need to learn."

He walked away.

Bruce remained under the dark screen with his fists clenched inside the pockets of his coat. He had not intended cruelty. That made it worse. Cruelty born of anger was at least an honest failure. Cruelty delivered as analysis could disguise itself as wisdom forever.

Anika Bell approached from the direction of C18 with a bundle of blankets.

"You two finished having the rich-man equivalent of a fistfight?" she asked.

Bruce looked at her. "I wasn't aware there was an audience."

"You were audible over the wind. Congratulations."

She handed him half the blankets. "Take these to the families at C12. Nobody there has enough."

"All right."

"And if you see Mr. Stark, give him a blanket too. He's standing there with a face like somebody kicked his lunch."

Bruce accepted the stack. He could not think of a useful response.

They walked in silence until Bell stopped outside the security checkpoint.

"For what it's worth," she said, "you both keep looking toward the same door. You could save time by arguing about how to open it instead of which of you cares more."

"It's not quite that simple."

"I know. You'd be surprised how much complicated things resemble simple things when you're cold."

She took her share of blankets toward the far waiting area. Bruce followed, carrying what she had given him, unsure whether he had learned anything or had merely been caught being foolish.

At C12 he helped an older couple unfold a blanket across two joined chairs. One of them thanked him; the other already slept. He saw Tony fifty feet away distributing water without looking up.

Their eyes met once.

Neither spoke.

In the corridor behind them, a tired maintenance worker wheeled a cart full of dry tools toward the north bridge. The sound of its wheels was small and determined, and it continued long after the two billionaires had decided they had nothing left to say.
EOF

# Chapter Thirteen: Under the Sign

The first sign Bruce had looked at in Bellwether International was a departures screen.

At 01:32, he found himself studying a sign painted directly onto the wall.

He and Asha stood in a deserted stretch of the central concourse. Most of its shops had pulled down their security grilles. Between a closed bookstore and an airline lounge, a decorative panel advertised the history of the airport: a black-and-white photograph of propeller aircraft against a low terminal, a picture of the first jet bridge, and a map showing the airport as it had been in 1978. Somewhere behind the display, a supply closet hummed on emergency power.

Asha had brought him there because of a notation on an old navigation index. The index referred to a zone marked C-0 in a font no longer used by the airport. The modern floor maps had no such designation.

"The computers don't agree with the walls," she said.

Bruce considered the faded map. The old terminal had been named Central before it was renamed Terminal C. Its observation gallery was marked **CENTRAL OVERLOOK — C/O**. A small line carried the abbreviation to an old service bridge.

"That's not zero," he said.

"It's the letter O. But the wayfinding archive had both spellings. When the old building was connected to the new one, somebody entered it as C0 in a database. No one ever needed it for passengers, so it stayed."

"A harmless error?"

"Until something decided it was a real destination."

She said *something* with the impatience of a person who had spent a year fixing small errors that other people insisted were too minor to matter.

"How did the C0 message get to the airline monitors?" Bruce asked.

"That's the part I went back to check. We have an emergency-message service that can ask several display vendors to show one notice. It exists for storms, fires, evacuations. VELA is allowed to submit a small list of preapproved shelter messages through it. But 'priority transfer—boarding' isn't on the approved list."

"Could somebody have added it tonight?"

"Possibly. But Evan Lowe found a training record from the pilot. The very same wording is in the sample transfer exercise. It was supposed to show only on contractor tablets. The rehearsal was postponed, but its message package was still queued in a staging process. They need to show us the records to know why it reached public displays."

"Would you call that a cause?"

"No. I'd call it a lead." She hesitated. "I heard you tell Mr. Stark not to mistake one for the other."

"He was unhappy about that."

"I know. We heard that too."

Bruce sighed. "Of course you did."

They turned toward the mural of the old gallery. The photograph showed a curved room filled with families watching planes taxi across a smaller airfield. A boy in a striped sweater stood at the window. A woman held up a camera. A man in an airport cap leaned against a brass railing, looking past the photographer toward something outside the frame.

"I used to come here with my grandfather," Asha said. "Not this particular version. The newer gallery before they closed it to the public. You could feel the jets in your ribs when they passed. When I was little I thought the building had a heartbeat."

"You became an engineer."

"Systems apprentice. If I pass the exams."

"Why airports?"

"They're the last places where strangers genuinely have to rely on one another. Everybody thinks the thing that makes an airport work is the aircraft. It isn't. It's the people who get everyone to the right aircraft in the right condition."

"That's a good answer."

"It's also what my mother said when she worked baggage. I'm not claiming originality."

The sound of footsteps approached. Tony was carrying two paper cups. He stopped a few feet away and looked from Asha to the wall.

"You're investigating a picture. Good. I was worried somebody had finally given you a workable map."

Asha took a cup. "Tea?"

"Something pretending to be. They were out of everything."

"You didn't have to bring me anything."

"I was passing."

He was lying. Bruce could see it in the way he held the remaining cup, careful not to look toward Bruce until he had established a legitimate reason to be there.

"What's the mural say?" Tony asked.

Asha explained. Tony listened without interruption. Bruce noticed that, too.

"C slash O," Tony said. "An old human abbreviation becomes a machine identifier. The software sees a field and assumes it's a place. Then a rehearsal package decides it's a boarding destination."

"Maybe," Asha said. "But we still need the actual integration record."

"Right." Tony nodded. "Good. We ask for the record."

Bruce looked at him. "You sound like me."

"I showered this morning. I assume it'll wear off."

Asha took her tea and stepped away to answer her radio. The corridor filled with the sound of wind pressing against the far windows. Tony and Bruce remained facing the mural.

"I was out of line," Bruce said.

Tony looked at him. "Which line? The one where I was a self-absorbed disaster, or the one where I was turning other people into extras?"

"Both."

"That's refreshingly specific."

"I was angry. I assumed too much about you."

Tony sipped his tea, made a face, and put the cup on a nearby counter. "I was angry too. I keep thinking if I don't throw myself at the problem, I'm not trying hard enough. I'm aware that's a questionable management strategy."

"You didn't need to agree with me."

"Don't worry. I mostly don't."

"You were right about one thing. There are moments when waiting for absolute certainty causes harm."

"And you were right that opening the wrong door in a storm might be worse."

For a moment they simply watched the people moving in the distance: a pair of custodians pushing clean blankets, an airfield officer walking with a first-aid responder, a parent carrying a sleeping child. Neither man felt compelled to define the exchange as friendship.

"We need both," Bruce said. "Urgency and restraint."

"That's your pitch for a buddy-cop movie?"

"No."

"Because I'm telling you right now, I'm not wearing the cheap sunglasses."

"You'd bring your own."

Tony smiled. "And you wouldn't take them off at night."

Asha returned, breathless. "Jonah's got a development. The dry bridge is cleared for a controlled entry team. Fire safety has approved a temporary opening under incident command. They want us at the north access station."

Both men started walking at once.

"Not running?" Tony asked Bruce.

"They know where we're going."

"Who are you and what have you done with the billionaire who respects procedure?"

"You're still talking."

"It's a vital organ."

When they reached the north corridor, the old sign above the shutter was visible in the newly restored half-light. It had been concealed behind a more recent safety placard. The edge of the old lettering showed through where the newer panel had peeled away.

**C/O — OBSERVATION GALLERY**.

Below it, in new lettering, the screen repeated its false promise: **ZONE CLEARED**.

Tony stood under the two signs and looked up.

"One was written by someone who knew where the room was," he said. "The other by somebody who knew what the form was supposed to say."

Bruce had no answer. This time the silence belonged to agreement.
EOF
